You are an accomplished researcher in machine learning. You are considering a new method described in "New Method" for the research theme provided in "Research Topic". "Related Works" is a list of research papers that are highly relevant to this new method.

# Research Topic
Improving efficiency of hyperparameter optimization

# New Method
{
    "Open Problems": "•  Hyperband / ASHA decide whether to keep or stop a training run by comparing raw, noisy intermediate scores.  \n•  This ignores the well-studied fact that most ML learning curves can be accurately extrapolated with 2-3 initial checkpoints (power-law, log-linear, etc.).  \n•  Using a full surrogate model (BOIL, DPL) is accurate but adds GP training or NN ensembles, which is heavyweight for low-budget scenarios.  ",
    "Methods": "Curve–Projected Successive Halving (CPSH)\n1.  Start with the standard Successive-Halving schedule (R, η) exactly as in ASHA.\n2.  After a configuration has produced the first m checkpoints (default m = 3), fit a one-parameter log-linear extrapolation  ŷ (t)=a+ b·log(t)  using ordinary least squares (cheap, closed form).  \n3.  Compute an optimistic estimate of the final score at max budget R:  ŷ_R  and its analytic standard error σ̂_R.\n4.  Replace the raw intermediate score used by SH with the Lower-Confidence projection  ỹ = ŷ_R  −  c·σ̂_R  (c=1.0 by default).  This simple UCB-style adjustment keeps exploration for very noisy runs.\n5.  Proceed with the normal SH comparison-and-prune logic using ỹ instead of the observed metric.\n6.  No global surrogate is stored; only two running sums per worker are required (∑log t, ∑y·log t).\n7.  At later rungs, re-fit using all collected points, but stop as soon as the projected improvement over the current incumbent is below ε (early global stopping).",
    "Experimental Setup": "Benchmarks:  CIFAR-10 (ResNet-18), Penn Treebank (LSTM), and an XGBoost tabular task.\nBaselines:  ASHA (PyTorch-TUNE default) and Hyperband-BOHB.\nBudgets:  same max epoch R and η=3 for all methods.\nMetrics:  (a) wall-clock time to reach the best-known validation score, (b) final test accuracy after exhausting the fixed resource budget.\nRuns:  10 seeds per method on a single 16-GPU node.",
    "Experimental Code": "# pseudo-python\nstate = {}\nfor config in sample_configs():\n    for t in rung_budgets():\n        y = train_one_step(config, t)\n        s = state.setdefault(config, [])\n        s.append((t, y))\n        if len(s) >= 3:\n            a,b = fit_log_linear(s)\n            yR, se = extrapolate(a,b,R)\n            proj = yR - se\n        else:\n            proj = y\n        report_to_scheduler(config, t, proj)",
    "Expected Result": "•  CPSH prunes 25-40 % more low-quality runs than ASHA while mis-pruning < 2 % of the ultimately best hyper-parameter configurations.\n•  Achieves the same final test accuracy as ASHA but with 30-35 % lower GPU hours; within 5 % of BOHB while being 3-4× faster in scheduler overhead.",
    "Expected Conclusion": "A tiny, drop-in modification—replacing raw rung scores with a one-line log-linear projection—captures most of the benefit of curve-aware Bayesian optimizers without their computational cost.  CPSH therefore offers a pragmatic middle ground for practitioners who already use Hyperband/ASHA but seek further efficiency gains."
}

# Related Works
{
    "Title": "Bayesian Optimization for Iterative Learning",
    "Main Contributions": "The paper presents Bayesian Optimization for Iterative Learning (BOIL), an approach to efficiently tune hyperparameters for deep (reinforcement) learning systems by exploiting their iterative training structure. It introduces learning an evaluation function to compress learning progress (training success and stability) from the entire training curve into a single numeric score, rather than relying solely on final performance. BOIL balances the benefit of additional training steps against computational cost and enhances model efficiency through a selective data augmentation technique for intermediate training information. The algorithm demonstrates superior performance in identifying optimal hyperparameters in minimal wall-clock time compared to existing baselines for DRL agents and CNNs.",
    "Methodology": "BOIL models the cost-sensitive black-box function as a Gaussian Process (GP) over a joint space of input hyperparameters and training iterations, using a product of square-exponential kernels for covariance. A linear regressor approximates the training time cost. The acquisition function is a modiﬁed Expected Improvement criterion, balancing exploration, exploitation, and low cost. Training curves are compressed into a numeric score using a Logistic (Sigmoid) preference function, whose growth and middle point parameters are learned by maximizing the GP log marginal likelihood. To avoid ill-conditioning, data augmentation is performed by actively selecting a subset of points from the training curve at maximum GP predictive uncertainty, with the number of augmented points adaptively controlled by a threshold on the GP covariance matrix condition number.",
    "Experimental Setup": "The algorithm was evaluated on two DRL agents (Dueling DQN on CartPole-v0, A2C on InvertedPendulum-v2 and Reacher-v2) and a Convolutional Neural Network (CNN) on two datasets (SVHN and CIFAR10). Experiments were conducted on an NVIDIA 1080 GTX GPU using TensorFlow-GPU, OpenAI Gym, and Mujoco. Results were averaged over 20 independent runs. Square-exponential kernels were used for the GP, with parameters optimized via marginal likelihood. Key settings included a maximum of 15 augmented points and a natural log condition number threshold of 20 for the GP covariance matrix. Baselines for comparison included Hyperband, Continuous Multi-task/Fidelity BO (CM-T/F-BO), vanilla BO, and BO with curve compression (BO-L). An ablation study also assessed the impact of different preference functions and an alternative time-kernel.",
    "Limitations": "The explicit limitations of the BOIL approach itself are not directly stated, but implicit ones can be inferred. The approach relies on a specific Sigmoid preference function for curve compression, which, while shown to be robust, is a learned approximation of user preference. The adaptive selection of augmented points uses a heuristic threshold for the GP covariance matrix condition number (δ=20), which might not be universally optimal. The cost function is approximated by a linear regressor, implying potential simplifications if the cost has more complex dependencies. The paper highlights limitations of other methods (e.g., traditional BO ignoring intermediate steps, Freeze-thaw BO being unsuitable for noisy DRL curves, Hyperband being misled by early fluctuations) which BOIL aims to overcome, suggesting these are the problems in the domain rather than drawbacks of BOIL.",
    "Future Research Directions": "The proposed BOIL framework is applicable to a broader range of machine learning systems beyond deep reinforcement learning and convolutional neural networks. Furthermore, the approach is not restricted to machine learning algorithms and can be generally applied to any process that exhibits an iterative structure. A concrete example mentioned is the 'optimization of manufacturing pipelines in which factory setting are adjusted to increase productivity'.",

= 0\n\n\nclass BOIL(object):\n\n    #def __init__(self, gp_params, func_params, acq_params, verbose=True):\n    def __init__(self, func, SearchSpace,acq_name=\"ei_mu_max\",verbose=1):\n\n       scalerT = MinMaxScaler()\n        SearchSpace_T=np.atleast_2d(self.SearchSpace[-1,:]).T\n        scalerT.fit(SearchSpace_T)\n\n        self.Xscaler=scaler\n        self.Tscaler=scalerT\n\n utility_cost_evaluation(self,x,acq_func,isDebug=False):\n        # this is a wrapper function to evaluate at multiple x(s)\n        \n        \n        def           \n            try:\n                mean_cost=self.linear_regression.predict(np.reshape(x,(1,-1)))\n                \n            except:\n                print(x)\n                print(\"bug\")\n    \n            mean_cost=max(0,mean_cost)+0.1 # to avoid <=0 cost\n            \n            #acquisition_function_value= utility_normalized/cost_normalized\n            if 'ei' in return acquisition_function_value*(-1) # since we will minimize this acquisition function\n        \n        \n        if len(x)==self.dim: # one observation\n            temp=utility_cost_evaluation_single(x,acq_func,isDebug=isDebug)\n            if isDebug==True:\n                return temp\n            else:\n                utility=np.mean(temp)\n        \n                                                    \n                utility[idx]=np.mean(temp)\n                \n            utility=np.asarray(utility)    \t\t\t               \n        return utility   \n    \n        \n    def acq_utility_cost(self):\n        \n        # generate a set of x* at T=MaxIter\n        # instead of running optimization on the whole space, we will only operate print(\"acq value =0\")\n            \n        return x_min\n    \n    \n    def select_informative_location_by_uncertainty(self,n_virtual_obs,x_max,t_max):\n        # this function will select a list     temp_T = np.vstack((temp_T, x_max_pred_variance[-1].reshape((1, -1)))) # append new t\n            temp_gp.X,temp_gp.T=temp_X,temp_T\n            temp_Y=np.random.random(size=(len(temp_T),1))\n       print(np.round(temp_y_original_whole_curve,decimals=4), np.round(y_virtual_original,decimals=4))\n#            \n        \n    def suggest_nextpoint(self): # logistic, time-cost, virtual\n        \"\"\"\n        Main optimization method.\n\n\n        Returns\n        -------\n        x: recommented point for evaluation\n        \"\"\"\n \n        # init a new Gaussian Process============================================\n        self.gp=ProductGaussianProcess(self.scaleSearchSpace,self.gp.hyper,self.gp.logistic_hyper)\n        self.gp.fit(self.X, self.gp.logistic_hyper['midpoint'],self.gp.logistic_hyper['growth']]\n            newlengthscale_x,newlengthscale_t,new_midpoint, new_growth = self.gp.hyper['lengthscale_t']=self.gp.hyper['lengthscale_t']\n            self.gp.logistic_hyper['midpoint']=new_midpoint\n            self.gp.logistic_hyper['growth']=new_growth\n          \n            if self.verbose:\n                print(\"==estimated lengthscale_x={:.4f}   lengthscale_t={:.3f}   Logistic_m0={:.1f}   Logistic_g0={:.1f}\".format(\n                    newlengthscale_x,newlengthscale_t,new_midpoint,new_growth))\n                \n        # Set acquisition function\n        start_opt=time.time()\n\n        # linear regression is used to fit the cost\n        # fit X and T\n        combine_input=np.hstack((self.X,self.T))\n        self.linear_regression.fit(combine_input,self.Y_cost)\n        \n        # maximize the acquisition function to select the next point =================================\n        x_max_temp=self.acq_utility_cost()\n        x_max=x_max_temp[:-1]\n        t_max=x_max_temp[-1]       \n            \n        # record keeping stuffs ====================================================\n        # record the optimization time\n        finished_opt=time.time()\n        elapse_opt=finished_opt-start_opt\n        self.time_opt=np.hstack((self.time_opt,elapse_opt))\n\n        # this is for house keeping stuff        \n        self.markVirtualObs.append(0)\n\n        self.X = np.vstack((self.X, x_max.reshape((1, -1))))\n        self.T = np.vstack((self.T, t_max.reshape((1, -1))))\n\n        # compute X in original scale\n        temp_X_new_original=self.Xscaler.inverse_transform(np.reshape(x_max,(-1,self.dim-1)))\n        #temp_X_new_original=x_max*self.max_min_gap[:-1]+self.SearchSpace[:-1,0]\n        self.X_original=np.vstack((self.X_original, temp_X_new_original))\n        \n        #temp_T_new_original=t_max*self.max_min_gap[-1]+self.SearchSpace[-1,0]\n  x_original_to_test=x_max_temp*self.max_min_gap+self.SearchSpace[:,0]\n\n        # evaluate the black-box function=================================================\n        y_original_curves, self.gp.logistic_hyper['midpoint'],self.gp.logistic_hyper['growth'],self.SearchSpace[-1,1])\n        \n        if len(y_original_curves)==1: # list\n            self.Y_curves.append(y_original_curves[0])\n        else:\n            self.Y_curves.append(y_original_curves)\n\n        \n        self.Y_original = np.append(self.Y_original,y_original)\n self.generating_virtual_observations(x_max,t_max,y_original_curves,y_cost_original[0])\n        \n        # update Y after change Y_original        \n        if np.std(self.Y_original)==0:\n            self.Y=(self.Y_original-np.mean(self.Y_original))\n        else:\n            self.Y=(self.Y_original-np.mean(self.Y_original))/np.std(self.Y_original)\n            \n     "Experiment Result": "The BOIL method uses a Product Gaussian Process (PGP) for modeling the cost-sensitive black-box function and a `linear_model.LinearRegression` from `sklearn` for approximating training time cost. The acquisition function is a modified Expected Improvement (`ei_mu_max`) criterion that balances exploration, exploitation, and low cost, formulated as `log(utility) - log(mean_cost)` and minimized as `(-1) * (log(utility) - log(mean_cost))`. Training curves are compressed into a numeric score using a Logistic (Sigmoid) preference function.\n\n**Gaussian Process (ProductGaussianProcess) Settings:**\n*   **Kernel:** Product of two RBF (Squared-Exponential) kernels: one for input hyperparameters (`x`) and one for training iterations (`t`).\n*   **Noise:** Initial `noise_delta` is `5e-4`.\n*   **Hyperparameter Optimization:** Performed every `2 * dim` iterations (where `dim` is the total number of dimensions in the search space, including training iterations). The hyperparameters optimized are `lengthscale_x`, `lengthscale_t`, `midpoint`, and `growth` by maximizing the GP log marginal likelihood.\n*   **Optimizer for GP Hyperparameters:** `L-BFGS-B` method with `maxiter=30*dim` and `maxfun=30*dim` for multi-start optimization.\n*   **Lengthscale Bounds for Optimization:** `lengthscale_x` in `[0.03, 0.3]`, `lengthscale_t` in `[0.3, 0.6]` (approximately `10 * min_lengthscale_x` to `2 * max_lengthscale_x`).\n\n**Training Curve Compression (Logistic Preference Function) Settings:**\n*   **Function:** `1.0 / (1 + np.exp(-growth * (x - midpoint)))`.\n*   **Parameters:** `midpoint` (initial 0.0, optimized) and `growth` (initial 1.0, optimized).\n*   **Parameter Bounds for Optimization:** `midpoint` in `[-2, 3]`, `growth` in `[0.5, 2]`.\n*   **Score Calculation:** The numeric score is the mean of the product of the raw performance curve and the scaled logistic function values, from a `threshold` point determined by the `midpoint` parameter.\n\n**Cost Model Settings:**\n*   **Approximation:** `sklearn.linear_model.LinearRegression`.\n*   **Input:** Scaled input hyperparameters (`X`) and scaled training iterations (`T`).\n*   **Cost Transformation:** The predicted mean cost is transformed using `max(0, mean_cost) + 0.1` (to avoid non-positive values) and then `np.log(transformed_mean_cost)` for use in the acquisition function.\n\n**Acquisition Function Settings:**\n*   **Type:** Modified Expected Improvement (`ei_mu_max`), where the incumbent value `y_max` is set to `mu_max_val` (the maximum of the GP's predictive mean).\n*   **Criterion:** `np.log(utility) - np.log(mean_cost)` (equivalent to `log(utility/cost)`), which is then minimized as `(-1) * (np.log(utility) - np.log(mean_cost))`.\n*   **Optimizer:** `L-BFGS-B` method for `acq_min_scipy_kwargs` with `maxiter=10*dim` and `maxfun=20*dim`, using multi-start initialization with `50*dim` random points.\n\n**Data Augmentation (Virtual Observations) Settings:**\n*   **Strategy:** For each new real observation, a subset of points (up to `self.max_n_augmentation=10`) is actively selected from the training curve at maximum GP predictive uncertainty. This uncertainty is evaluated using the `pure_exploration` acquisition function.\n*   **Adaptive Control:** Augmentation stops if the `log(condition_number)` of the GP covariance matrix exceeds `self.threshold_cond=15` or if the predictive variance `pred_var_value` drops below `(self.gp.noise_delta + 1e-3)`.\n*   **Virtual Point Generation:** The selected virtual points for `(x, t_virtual)` have their y-values calculated by applying the `transform_logistic` function to the observed curve truncated at `t_virtual`. The cost for these virtual points is linearly interpolated based on the cost of the full run: `y_cost_original * virtual_obs_t_scaled`.\n\n**Initialization:**\n*   `n_init_points=3` initial random points are evaluated.\n*   Virtual observations are generated for each initial point. \n*   Input hyperparameters are scaled to `[0,1]` using `MinMaxScaler`."

    "Title": "Hyperparameter Optimization through Neural Network Partitioning",
    "Main Contributions": "This paper proposes a simple and efficient method for hyperparameter optimization called \"partitioned neural networks.\" Inspired by the marginal likelihood, this approach optimizes hyperparameters without requiring a separate validation set and within a single training run. It addresses challenges in scenarios with limited data or high computational costs, such as federated learning, by partitioning the training data and the neural network model into K data shards and parameter partitions, respectively. The method defines an \"out-of-training-sample\" loss on unseen data shards as the objective for hyperparameter optimization, demonstrating significantly lower computational cost compared to alternative marginal likelihood-based methods.",
    "Methodology": "The core methodology involves partitioning a neural network's parameters into C partitions (w1, ..., wC) and the training data into C shards (D1, ..., DC). Subnetworks w(k)s are constructed by combining the first k parameter partitions and setting the remaining parameters to default values (e.g., initialization values). Each partition wk is optimized on data shards D1:k using its corresponding subnetwork w(k)s. Hyperparameters ψ are optimized using a marginal likelihood-inspired objective LML, which sums the 'out-of-sample' losses of subnetworks. This objective, LML(D,ψ) = sum_{k=1 to C} E_{qk-1(w)}[log p(Dk|w,ψ)], serves as an approximation to a lower-bound on the marginal likelihood. The optimization uses stochastic gradient descent, with interleaved updates for model parameters and hyperparameters. Random weight partitioning is used where a fixed proportion of weights in each layer is randomly assigned to a partition. The method ensures that a subnetwork trained on D1:k uses only parameters updated on that data subset.",
    "Experimental Setup": "The method was evaluated on several tasks and datasets. For input selection, a toy dataset with 15 informative and 15 spurious features was used with fully connected MLPs. For learning invariances through data augmentations, experiments were conducted on MNIST, CIFAR10, TinyImagenet, and their rotated variants (rotCIFAR10, rotTinyImagenet, rotMNIST). Architectures included CNNs for MNIST, fixupResNets (8 and 14 layers) for CIFAR10, and ResNet-50 (with GroupNorm) for TinyImagenet. Baselines included standard training (no augmentations), Augerino, Differentiable Laplace, and Last-layer Marginal Likelihood. Performance was also compared against traditional training/validation splits with fine-tuning using a Wide ResNet-20 on CIFAR10. In Federated Learning, experiments were performed on non-i.i.d. splits of MNIST, rotMNIST, CIFAR10, and rotCIFAR10 across 100 clients, using a CNN for MNIST and a ResNet-9 with GroupNorm for CIFAR10. Validation accuracy was the primary metric, averaged over 10 evaluations.",
    "Limitations": "The method's inherent design requires an additional forward-backward pass to update hyperparameters, leading to some computational overhead, though it is significantly less than existing work. Training partitioned networks generally requires more iterations to converge. Partitioning the network inherently constrains its capacity, which can lead to some loss of performance compared to a full, non-partitioned network trained with optimally tuned hyperparameters. Additionally, the method introduces new hyperparameters related to the partitioning strategy itself, specifically choosing the number of chunks (C), and the relative proportions of data and parameters assigned to each chunk, which may require tuning for optimal performance.",
    "Future Research Directions": "Future research could explore dynamic partitioning of network parameters during training. Investigating alternative partitioning schemes beyond random weight partitioning (e.g., node partitioning) to potentially allow for single-batch updates for multiple partitions is another direction. Optimizing the scheduling of hyperparameter updates (e.g., accumulating gradients from different chunks or interleaving updates at less frequent intervals) to reduce computational overhead and variance per update is also mentioned. Addressing the potential performance loss due to network partitioning by adjusting training rounds or increasing initial network capacity is a relevant area for future work.",
    "Experiment Code": null,
    "Experiment Result": null

    "Title": "Implicit differentiation of Lasso-type models for hyperparameter optimization",
    "Main Contributions": "The paper introduces an efficient implicit differentiation algorithm for hyperparameter optimization of Lasso-type models, addressing challenges posed by non-smooth loss functions and high computational/memory costs of existing gradient methods. Key contributions include proving that forward iterative differentiation of block coordinate descent converges linearly to the true gradient once the support is identified, proposing an algorithm that decouples Jacobian computation from regression coefficients and avoids solving ill-conditioned linear systems, and demonstrating superior performance against state-of-the-art hyperparameter optimization methods on both held-out validation and Stein Unbiased Risk Estimator (SURE) criteria, scaling effectively to high-dimensional data and numerous hyperparameters.",
    "Methodology": "The methodology casts hyperparameter optimization as a bi-level optimization problem that can be solved by gradient descent in the hyperparameter space. It focuses on efficiently estimating the gradient with respect to hyperparameters (Jacobian). The proposed \"Implicit Forward Iterative Differentiation\" (Algorithm 2) leverages the fixed-point iteration induced by proximal Block Coordinate Descent (BCD) algorithms for Lasso-type models. It differentiates this fixed-point equation to derive sparse closed-form solutions for the Jacobian (Proposition 1). The algorithm operates in two decoupled steps: first solving the inner Lasso problem to identify the support of the regression coefficients, then iteratively computing the Jacobian by restricting updates to this identified support. This approach avoids direct matrix inversion, which can be computationally expensive and numerically unstable for large or ill-conditioned systems, and ensures linear convergence of the Jacobian once the support is identified (Proposition 2).",
    "Experimental Setup": "The method was implemented in Python using Numba for performance-critical sections. It was benchmarked against several hypergradient-based competitors (Implicit Differentiation, Forward Iterative Differentiation) and non-gradient-based methods (Grid-search, Random-search, Lattice Hyp., Bayesian optimization). For fair comparison, a vanilla BCD algorithm (Algorithm 5) was used for all inner optimization problems, with a stopping tolerance of 10^-5. Hypergradient methods employed a line-search strategy, and initialization strategies were adapted for Lasso and weighted Lasso (e.g., `λmax - log(10)` for Lasso, a regularized bi-level problem solution for weighted Lasso). Hyperparameter optimization was evaluated using held-out loss (datasets: rcv1, 20news, finance, split into train/validation/test) and the Stein Unbiased Risk Estimator (SURE) with a finite difference Monte-Carlo approximation (synthetic data: `n=100`, `p` varied from 200 to 10,000, i.i.d. Gaussian design, sparse ground truth `β*`, `SNR=3`). Metrics included time to compute gradients, objective value distance to optimum, loss on test set, and relative Mean Squared Error.",
    "Limitations": "The standard implicit differentiation approach is not applicable to non-smooth problems due to reliance on smooth optimality conditions. While generally robust, the theoretical guarantees of the proposed method (Propositions 1 and 2) assume uniqueness of the Lasso solution, which may not hold in pathological cases (though such cases are rare and often have zero Lebesgue measure). The current theory does not cover non-convex penalty functions (e.g., MCP), although empirical results showed good performance. The overall bi-level optimization problem for hyperparameter selection is typically non-convex, meaning convergence to a global minimum is not guaranteed and is sensitive to initialization. The SURE criterion requires knowledge of the noise variance. Backward iterative differentiation was found to be orders of magnitude slower and more memory-intensive in experiments. The classical implicit differentiation can suffer from slow convergence when solving ill-conditioned linear systems.",
    "Future Research Directions": "Future work could focus on extending the theoretical framework of implicit differentiation to explicitly cover non-convex penalty functions beyond Lasso-type models, such as MCP or Elastic-Net. Another promising direction involves leveraging the two-step nature of the proposed algorithm to integrate with advanced state-of-the-art Lasso solvers that use techniques like active sets or screening rules. These solvers introduce discontinuities with respect to hyperparameters, which currently pose significant challenges for single-step automatic differentiation approaches, but could potentially be handled by the decoupled methodology.",
    "Experiment Code": "class ImplicitForward():\n    \"\"\"Algorithm to compute the hypergradient using implicit forward\n    differentiation.\n\n    First the algorithm computes the regression coefficients.\n    Then the iterations of the forward differentiation are applied to compute\n    the Jacobian.\n\n    Parameters\n    ----------\n    tol_jac: float\n        Tolerance for the Jacobian computation.\n    max_iter: int\n        Maximum number of iterations for the inner solver.\n    n_iter_jac: int\n        Maximum number of iterations for the Jacobian computation.\n    use_stop_crit: bool, optional (default=True)\n        Use stopping criterion in hypergradient computation.      self.use_stop_crit = use_stop_crit\n        self.verbose = verbose\n\n    def get_beta_jac(\n            self, X, y, log_alpha, model, get_grad_outer, mask0=None,\n            dense0=None,   jac_v = model.get_full_jac_v(mask, jac_v, X.shape[1])\n\n        return mask, dense, jac_v, jac\n\n\ndef get_bet_jac_implicit_forward(\n        X, y, log_alpha, model, mask0=None, dense0=None, jac0=None,\n        tol=1e-3, max_iter=1000, niter_jac=1000, tol_jac=1e-6, verbose=False,\n        use_stop_crit=True):\n\n    mask, dense, _ = compute_beta(\n        X, y, log_alpha, mask0=mask0, model=\"lasso\", mask=None, dense=None, verbose=False,\n        use_stop_crit=True):\n    n_samples, n_features = Xs.shape\n\n    L = model.get_L(Xs)\n\n    residual_norm = []\n\n    if hasattr(model, model._init_dbeta(n_features)\n        ddual_var = model._init_ddual_var(dbeta, Xs, y, sign_beta, alpha)\n\n    for i in range(niter_jac):\n        if verbose:\n            print(\"%i -st iterations over %i\" % (i, niter_jac))\n        if issparse(Xs):\n            model._update_only_jac_sparse(\n                Xs.data, Xs.indptr, Xs.indices, y, n_samples,\n                n_features, dbeta,           model.get_jac_residual_norm(\n                Xs, y, n_samples, sign_beta, dbeta, dual_var,\n                ddual_var, alpha))\n        if use_stop_crit and i > 1:\n            # relative stopping criterion for the computation of the jacobian\n            # and absolute stopping criterion to handle warm start\n            rel_tol = np.abs(residual_norm[-2] - residual_norm[-1])\n get_only_jac.n_iter = i\n\n    return dbeta\n\n\ndef compute_beta(\n        X, y, log_alpha, model, mask0=None, dense0=None, jac0=None,\n        max_iter=1000, tol=1e-3, compute_jac=True, return_all=False,\n        save_iterates=False, verbose=False, use_stop_crit=True, gap_freq=10):\n    \"\"\"\n    Parameters\n    --------------\n    X: array-like, shape (n_samples, n_features)\n        Design matrix.\n    y: ndarray, shape (n_samples,)\n        Observation vector.\n    log_alpha: float or np.array, shape (n_features,)\n        Logarithm of hyperparameter.\n    beta0: ndarray, shape (n_features,)\n        initial value of the regression coefficients\n        beta for warm start\n    dbeta0: ndarray, shape (n_features,)\n        initial value of the jacobian dbeta for warm start\n    max_iter: int\n        number of iterations of the algorithm\n    tol: float\n        The tolerance for the optimization: if the updates are\n        smaller than ``tol``, the optimization code checks the\n        primal decrease for optimality and continues until it\n        is smaller than ``tol``\n    compute_jac: bool\n        to compute or not the Jacobian along with the regression\n        coefficients\n    model:  instance of ``sparse_ho.base.BaseModel``\n        A model that follows the sparse_ho API.\n    return_all: bool\n        to store the iterates or not in order to compute the Jacobian in a\n        backward way\n    use_stop_crit: bool\n        use a stopping criterion or do all the iterations\n    gap_freq : int\n        After how many passes on the data the dual gap should be computed\n        to stop the iterations.\n\n    Returns\n    -------\n    mask : ndarray, shape (n_features,)\n        The mask of non-zero coefficients in beta.\n    dense : ndarray, shape (n_nonzeros,)\n        The beta coefficients on the support\n    jac : ndarray, shape (n_nonzeros,) or (n_nonzeros, q)\n        The jacobian restricted to the support. If there are more than\n        one hyperparameter then it has two dimensions.\n    \"\"\"\n    n_samples, n_features = X.shape\n    is_sparse = issparse(X)\n    if not is_sparse and not np.isfortran(X):\n        X = np.asfortranarray(X)\n    L = model.get_L(X)\n\n    ############################################\n    alpha = np.exp(log_alpha)\n\n    if hasattr(model, 'estimator') and model.estimator is not None:\n        return model._use_estimator(X, y, alpha, tol)\n\n    try:\n        alpha.shape[0]\n        alphas = alpha.copy()\n    except Exception:\n        alphas = np.ones(n_features) * alpha\n    ############################################\n    # warm start for beta\n    beta, dual_var = model._init_beta_dual_var(X, y, mask0, dense0)\n    ############################################\n    # warm start for dbeta\n    dbeta, ddual_var = model._init_dbeta_ddual_var(\n        X, y, mask0=mask0, dense0=dense0, jac0=jac0, compute_jac=compute_jac)\n\n    # store the values of the objective\n    pobj0 = model._get_pobj0(dual_var, np.zeros(X.shape[1]), alphas, y)\n    pobj = []\n\n    ############################################\n    # store the iterates if needed\n    if return_all:\n        list_beta = []\n    if save_iterates:\n        list_beta = []\n        list_jac = []\n\n    for i in range(max_iter):\n        if verbose:\n            print(\"%i -st iteration over %i\" % (i, max_iter))\n        if is_sparse:\n            model._update_beta_jac_bcd_sparse(\n                X.data, X.indptr, X.indices, y, n_samples, n_features, beta,\n                dbeta, dual_var, ddual_var, alphas, L,\n                compute_jac=compute_jac)\n        else:\n            model._update_beta_jac_bcd(\n                X, y, beta, dbeta, dual_var, ddual_var, alphas,\n                L, compute_jac=compute_jac)\n\n        pobj.append(model._get_pobj(dual_var, X, beta, alphas, y))\n\n        if i > 1:\n            if verbose:\n                print(\"relative decrease = \", (pobj[-2] - pobj[-1]) / pobj0)\n\n        if use_stop_crit and i % gap_freq == 0 and i > 0:\n            if hasattr(model, \"_get_dobj\"):\n                dobj = model._get_dobj(dual_var, X, beta, alpha, y)\n                dual_gap = pobj[-1] - dobj\n                if verbose:\n                    print(\"dual gap %.2e\" % dual_gap)\n                if verbose:\n                    print(\"gap %.2e\" % dual_gap)\n                if dual_gap < pobj0 * tol:\n                    break\n            else:\n                if (pobj[-2] - pobj[-1] <= pobj0 * tol):\n                    break\n        if return_all:\n            list_beta.append(beta.copy())\n        if save_iterates:\n            list_beta.append(beta.copy())\n            list_jac.append(dbeta.copy())\n    else:\n        if verbose:\n            print('did not converge !')\n\n    mask = beta != 0\n    dense = beta[mask]\n    jac = model._get_jac(dbeta, mask)\n    if hasattr(model, 'dual'):\n        model.dual_var = dual_var\n        if compute_jac:\n            model.ddual_var = ddual_var\n    if save_iterates:\n        return np.array(list_beta), np.array(list_jac)\n    if return_all:\n        return mask, dense, list_beta\n    else:\n        if compute_jac:\n            return mask, dense, jac\n        else:\n            return mask, dense, None\n\n\n\n@njit\ndef _update_beta_jac_bcd_sparse(\n        data, indptr, indices, y, n_samples, n_features, beta,\n        dbeta, dual_var, ddual_var, alphas, L, compute_jac=True):\n\n    non_zeros = np.where(L != 0)[0]\n\n    for j in non_zeros:\n        # get the j-st column of X in sparse format\n        Xjs = data[indptr[j]:indptr[j+1]]\n        # get the non zero indices\n        idx_nz = indices[indptr[j]:indptr[j+1]]\n        beta_old = beta[j]\n        if compute_jac:\n            dbeta_old = dbeta[j]\n        zj = beta[j] + dual_var[idx_nz] @ Xjs / (L[j] * n_samples)\n        beta[j:j+1] = ST(zj, alphas[j] / L[j])\n        if compute_jac:\n            dzj = dbeta[j] + Xjs @ ddual_var[idx_nz] / (L[j] * n_samples)\n            dbeta[j:j+1] = np.abs(np.sign(beta[j])) * dzj\n            dbeta[j:j+1] -= alphas[j] * np.sign(beta[j]) / L[j]\n            # update residuals\n            ddual_var[idx_nz] -= Xjs * (dbeta[j] - dbeta_old)\n        dual_var[idx_nz] -= Xjs * (beta[j] - beta_old)\n\n@staticmethod\n@njit\ndef _update_only_jac_sparse(\n        data, indptr, indices, y, n_samples, n_features,\n        dbeta, dual_var, ddual_var, L, alpha, sign_beta):\n    for j in range(n_features):\n        # get the j-st column of X in sparse format\n        Xjs = data[indptr[j]:indptr[j+1]]\n        # get the non zero idices\n        idx_nz = indices[indptr[j]:indptr[j+1]]\n        # store old beta j for fast update\n        dbeta_old = dbeta[j]\n        # update of the Jacobian dbeta\n        dbeta[j] += Xjs @ ddual_var[idx_nz] / (L[j] * n_samples)\n        dbeta[j] -= alpha * sign_beta[j] / L[j]\n        ddual_var[idx_nz] -= Xjs * (dbeta[j] - dbeta_old)",
    "Experiment Result": "The methodology is evaluated across various machine learning models, datasets, and optimization strategies, primarily focusing on its \"Implicit Forward Iterative Differentiation\" (Algorithm 2) for hyperparameter optimization.\n\n**Models:**\n- Lasso, ElasticNet, Weighted Lasso (Lasso-type models, `sparse_ho.models.Lasso`, `ElasticNet`, `WeightedLasso`)\n- Sparse Logistic Regression (`sparse_ho.models.SparseLogreg`)\n- Support Vector Machine (SVM, `sparse_ho.models.SVM`)\n- Support Vector Regression (SVR, `sparse_ho.models.SVR`), including Simplex SVR (`sparse_ho.models.SimplexSVR`)\n\n**Datasets:**\n- Real-world datasets: `rcv1` / `rcv1.binary` / `rcv1_train` (binary classification), `real-sim`, `leukemia`, `20news` / `20newsgroups`, `finance`, `mnist` (multiclass), `usps` (multiclass), `sector_scale` (multiclass), `aloi` (multiclass), `gina_agnostic`, `gisette`, `covtype`, MEG data.\n- Synthetic datasets: `simu` (generated with `make_classification` or `make_correlated_data`).\n\n**Criteria for Hyperparameter Optimization (Outer Objective):**\n- Held-out Mean Squared Error (HeldOutMSE)\n- Held-out Logistic Loss (HeldOutLogistic)\n- Cross-Validation (CrossVal), typically 5-fold (`KFold`)\n- Finite-Difference Monte-Carlo SURE (FiniteDiffMonteCarloSure), used for noisy regression with Lasso.\n\n**Outer Optimization Algorithms:**\n- Gradient Descent (GradientDescent): Uses an adaptive step size (`p_grad_norm`, `step_size`).\n- Line Search (LineSearch): Gradient descent with line search.\n- Adam (Adam).\n- Zero-order methods for comparison:\n    - Grid Search (`sparse_ho.grid_search.grid_search`)\n    - Random Search (`hyperopt_wrapper` with `method='random'`)\n    - Bayesian Optimization (`hyperopt_wrapper` with `method='bayesian'`)\n\n**Inner Optimization Settings (for `compute_beta` and Jacobian):**\n- **Algorithm:** `ImplicitForward` is the primary method, also compared with `Forward` and `Implicit` (implicit differentiation solving a linear system).\n- **Tolerance for inner problem (beta computation):** `tol` (e.g., 1e-3, 1e-5, 1e-6, 1e-8, 1e-16, 1e-32).\n- **Maximum iterations for inner problem:** `max_iter` (e.g., 50, 100, 1000, 10_000, 50_000, 100_000).\n- **Tolerance for Jacobian computation:** `tol_jac` (e.g., 1e-3, 1e-6, 1e-8, 1e-10, 1e-11, 1e-13, 1e-14, 1e-32).\n- **Maximum iterations for Jacobian computation:** `n_iter_jac` (e.g., 100, 500, 1000, 2000, 5000, 100_000).\n- **Warm-starting:** Iterates (`mask0`, `dense0`) and Jacobian (`quantity_to_warm_start` / `jac0`) are warm-started across outer iterations.\n- **Support identification:** The method relies on identifying the support of regression coefficients.\n\n**Hyperparameter Ranges and Initialization:**\n- Regularization parameters (`alpha` for Lasso/LogReg, `alpha_1`/`alpha_2` for ElasticNet, `C`/`epsilon` for SVM/SVR) are typically initialized as a fraction of `alpha_max` (the smallest value for which all coefficients are zero).\n- Ranges are often `np.geomspace` from `alpha_max` down to `alpha_max / 100` or `1 / 100_000`, `1 / 1_000_000` (or their log-equivalents).\n\n**Experimental Setup Highlights:**\n- **Comparison with CvxpyLayer:** `expes/expe_cvxpy/main_cvxpy.py` evaluates the accuracy and computation time of hypergradients against `CvxpyLayer` for Lasso and ElasticNet, demonstrating `sparse-ho`'s efficiency.\n- **Linear Convergence Analysis:** `expes/expe_linear_convergence/main_clean.py` and `main_svm.py` investigate the linear convergence of both inner problem solutions (`beta`) and their Jacobians (`J`) for Lasso, Logistic Regression, and SVM, often highlighting convergence after support identification.\n- **Hypergradient Computation Time:** `expes/hypergradient/main_hypergradient.py` and `main_hypergradient_svm.py` specifically measure the time required to compute hypergradients for varying numbers of inner iterations (`maxit`), comparing different differentiation algorithms.\n- **Multiclass Logistic Regression:** `expes/multiclass/main_multiclass.py` applies the methodology to multiclass problems, comparing against `sklearn.linear_model.LogisticRegression` (one-vs-rest strategy) and `hyperopt`.\n- **Real-world Applications:** Examples demonstrate use cases like comparing optimizers on `rcv1.binary`, Elastic Net for `rcv1` or `simu` data, LassoCV, Weighted Lasso on MEG data, and monitoring custom metrics.\n- **Plotting:** Extensive plotting scripts (`figure_*.py`, `plot_*.py`) visualize optimization paths, objective values, hypergradient convergence, and comparisons."

    "Title": "A New Linear Scaling Rule for Private Adaptive Hyperparameter Optimization",
    "Main Contributions": "The paper addresses the open problem of hyperparameter optimization (HPO) in differentially private deep learning, which is typically computationally expensive and difficult to account for privacy cost. It proposes a novel adaptive HPO method called the 'new linear scaling rule' that uses low-cost trials (small privacy budgets) to estimate optimal hyperparameters and scales them up for the final run. This method significantly reduces computation and privacy cost while achieving state-of-the-art performance across 22 benchmark tasks in computer vision and natural language processing, across a wide range of privacy budgets (ε ∈ [0.01, 8.0]), and diverse architectures.",
    "Methodology": "The core methodology is the 'new linear scaling rule'. It involves: 1) Reducing the dimensionality of HPO by combining key hyperparameters (learning rate η and number of steps T) into a single scalar variable `r = η × T`. This is motivated by theoretical analysis showing the excess empirical risk of private GD is related to a 'noisy radius' bounded by `η × T`. 2) Approximating the optimal `r(ε)` using a first-order Taylor approximation (linear interpolation). This is done by performing a small number of 'cheap trials' with small privacy budgets (e.g., ε1 = 0.1, ε2 = 0.2) using random search to find empirical points `(ε, r(ε))`, then fitting a line to these points to estimate `r` for any target `εf`. 3) Utilizing Differentially Private Gradient Descent (DP-GD) with full-batch gradient computation to maximize the signal-to-noise ratio, unit clipping (C=1), zero initialization of linear classifiers, momentum (ρ=0.9), and Privacy Loss Variable (PLV) accounting for tighter privacy guarantees.",
    "Experimental Setup": "The method was extensively evaluated on 22 benchmark tasks across various domains: Computer Vision (ImageNet, CIFAR10, CIFAR100, FashionMNIST, STL10, EMNIST, and distribution shift datasets like Waterbirds, FMoW, Camelyon17, CIFAR10→STL, CIFAR10p1, CIFAR10C, CIFAR100C) and Natural Language Processing (SQuAD, GLUE benchmark tasks: SST-2, QNLI, QQP, MNLI(m/mm), and language modeling tasks: PersonaChat, WikiText-2, Enron Emails). Models included ResNets and multi-billion-parameter Transformers (beit, beitv2, convnext, ViT-L, GPT-2, RoBERTa-base), covering pretraining and finetuning scenarios. Performance was compared against random search, grid search (without HPO privacy cost), and prior private HPO methods (Papernot & Steinke, Koskela & Kulkarni, Wang et al., DPAdamWOSM, Mehta et al.). Ablation studies were conducted on six design choices: architecture/initialization, batch size, optimizer, accounting method, and clipping norm. Evaluation metrics included accuracy for classification and perplexity for language modeling.",
    "Limitations": "The theoretical assumptions (e.g., strong convexity, smoothness, Lipschitzness, bounded gradients) do not hold true in all neural network settings, though the heuristic proved empirically successful even for complex Transformers. The adaptive HPO method has a higher runtime than simple random search and worse parallelization than grid search (requiring serial runs for epsilon dependencies). While the method typically uses 7 runs (3 for each initial epsilon, 1 final), it's more than random search's 1 run. The current approach fits a 1-D polynomial with 2 points, and while higher-degree polynomials could be used, they incur more privacy cost for HPO. The paper notes that tuning the hyperparameters of the HPO method itself (e.g., budget for small trials, number of points) is not explored. Optimizing additional hyperparameters like batch size or clipping threshold jointly with `η` and `T` degraded performance in tested scenarios.",
    "Future Research Directions": "Future work could incorporate additional factors into the theoretical analysis, such as momentum acceleration and clipping bias, or extend the analysis to more general neural networks. An interesting question is whether Renyi Differential Privacy (RDP) analysis can be applied to this adaptive method. The paper also suggests privatizing other non-private HPO methods. Exploring the application of this method to alternate threat models where a small percentage of private data is used during pretraining, or where public data assists private model training, is another direction. Further study with longer iterations for language modeling tasks is suggested. The robustness of the rule that 'combinations of `η, T` that evaluate to the same product perform similarly' could be further investigated, particularly regarding its implications for fitting more complex functions than a linear one.",
    "Experiment Code": "class PrivacyEngine(object):\n    \"\"\"Differentially-private optimization engine that works gracefully with Hugging Face transformers.\n\n    Supports ghost clipping as described in\n        Li, X., Tramèr, F., Liang, P., & Hashimoto, T. (2021).\n        Large Language Models Can Be Strong Differentially Private Learners.\n        arXiv preprint arXiv:2110.05679.\n\n    Implicitly assumes inputs are in batch first format.\n    \"\"\"\n\n    def __init__(\n        self,\n        module: nn.Module,\n        *,\n        batch_size: int,\n        sample_size: int,\n        max_grad_norm: float,\n        epochs: Optional[Union[int, float]] = None,\n        noise_multiplier: Optional[float] = None,\n        target_epsilon: Optional[float] = None,\n        target_delta: Optional[float] = None,\n        alphas: Sequence[float] = accounting_manager.DEFAULT_ALPHAS,\n        record_snr: bool = True,\n        named_params: Optional[Sequence] = None,\n        numerical_stability_constant=1e-6,\n        clipping_mode=ClippingMode.default,\n        accounting_mode=\"rdp\",\n        eps_error=0.05,\n        skip_checks=False,\n        **unused_kwargs,\n    ):\n        \"\"\"Initialize the engine.\n\n        Args:\n            module: The PyTorch module for which per-sample gradient is required.\n                Setting the `requires_grad` attribute of a parameter to False\n                disables the per-sample gradient accumulation.\n            batch_size: The expected size of Poisson-sampled batch, i.e., the lot size.\n            sample_size: Size of dataset.\n            max_grad_norm: The maximum 2-norm for gradient clipping.\n            epochs: The number of epochs for training.\n            noise_multiplier: The extra multiplier for DP-SGD noise.\n            target_epsilon: The target privacy spending.\n                Only used to estimate the `noise_multiplier` if it is not set.\n            target_delta: The target failure probability.\n                Defaults to sample_size ** -1.1 if not set.\n            alphas: The RDP orders for (ε, δ)-DP conversion. Useless if not accounting in RDP.\n            record_snr: Record and report the signal-to-noise ratio --\n                ratio between norm of summed clipped gradient and norm of noise vector.\n            named_params: Specifies which parameters need gradients;\n                defaults to use parameters which require grad in module.\n            numerical_stability_constant: Small constant to avoid division by 0 when clipping.\n            clipping_mode: The clipping mode to use. One of 'default', 'ghost', 'per_layer', 'per_layer_percentile'.\n            accounting_mode: The method of accounting privacy. One of (`rdp`, `glw`, `all`).\n                Meanings of shorthands:\n                    - rdp: Account loss with RDP but perform conversion to approx-DP with a procedure defined in\n                        \"The Discrete Gaussian for Differential Privacy\". https://arxiv.org/abs/2004.00010\n                    - glw: Account loss by numerically composing tradeoff functions in f-DP; defined in\n                        \"Numerical composition of differential privacy\". https://arxiv.org/abs/2106.02848\n                    - all: Report loss with all methods listed above.\n            eps_error: Error threshold for upper and lower bound in the GLW accounting procedure.\n            skip_checks: Skips the model type validation test if True.\n        \"\"\"\n        utils.handle_unused_kwargs(unused_kwargs)\n        del unused_kwargs\n        super(PrivacyEngine, self).__init__()\n\n        if clipping_mode not in ClippingMode.all():\n            raise ValueError(f\"Unknown clipping mode {clipping_mode}. Expected one of {ClippingMode.all()}.\")\n        if accounting_mode not in AccountingMode.all():\n            raise ValueError(f\"Unknown accounting mode: {accounting_mode}. Expected one of {AccountingMode.all()}.\")\n        if epochs <= 0.0:\n            raise ValueError(f\"Number of training epochs cannot be non-positive, but found epochs={epochs}\")\n\n        # Privacy parameters.\n        sample_rate = batch_size / sample_size\n        if target_delta is None:\n            target_delta = sample_size ** -1.1\n        if noise_multiplier is None:\n            if target_epsilon is None or epochs is None:\n                raise ValueError(\n                    f\"`target_epsilon` and `epochs` must be specified when `noise_multiplier` is `None`.\"\n                )\n            if accounting_mode in (\"rdp\", \"all\"):\n                manager = accounting_manager.RDPManager(alphas=alphas)\n            else:  # \"glw\"\n                manager = accounting_manager.GLWManager(eps_error=eps_error)\n            noise_multiplier = manager.compute_sigma(\n                target_epsilon=target_epsilon, target_delta=target_delta, sample_rate=sample_rate, epochs=epochs,\n            )\n\n        self.batch_size = batch_size\n        self.sample_size = sample_size\n        self.sample_rate = sample_rate\n        self.max_grad_norm = max_grad_norm\n\n        self.epochs = epochs\n        self.noise_multiplier = noise_multiplier\n        self.effective_noise_multiplier = noise_multiplier / batch_size\n        self.target_epsilon = target_epsilon\n        self.target_delta = target_delta\n        self.alphas = alphas\n        self.eps_error = eps_error\n        self.accounting_mode = accounting_mode\n        self.record_snr = record_snr\n\n        # Internals.\n        self.steps = 0  # Tracks privacy spending.\n\n        # Recording.\n        self.max_clip = None\n        self.min_clip = None\n        self.med_clip = None\n        self.signal = None\n        self.noise = None\n        self.snr = None\n        self.noise_limit = None\n\n        # Record parameters.\n        self.module = module\n        if named_params is None:\n            self.named_params = tuple(\n                (name, param) for (name, param) in module.named_parameters() if param.requires_grad\n            )\n        else:\n            self.named_params = named_params\n        self.num_params = sum(param.numel() for _, param in self.named_params)\n\n        self._locked = False  # Lock the part where noisy gradients is created (in `self.step`) if True.\n        self.numerical_stability_constant = numerical_stability_constant\n        self.clipping_mode = clipping_mode\n        if clipping_mode == ClippingMode.ghost:\n            autograd_grad_sample.set_hooks_mode(BackwardHookMode.ghost_norm)  # Prepare for first backward.\n        else:\n            autograd_grad_sample.set_hooks_mode(BackwardHookMode.default)  # Extra guard.\n\n        if not isinstance(module, SUPPORTED_TRANSFORMERS) and not skip_checks:\n            raise ValueError(\n                f\"Model type {type(module)} is not supported. Please file an issue if you want this model to be added.\\n\"\n                f\"Currently supported transformers are: {SUPPORTED_TRANSFORMERS}\"\n            )\n        transformers_support.forward_swapper(module=module)  # Fix the position embeddings broadcast issue.\n\n    def lock(self):\n        \"\"\"Run this after noisy clipped gradient is created to prevent tampering with it before parameter update.\"\"\"\n        self._locked = True\n\n    def unlock(self):\n        \"\"\"Run this after parameter update to allow creation of noisy gradient for next step\"\"\"\n        self._locked = False\n\n    def attach(self, optimizer):\n        # `loss_reduction=\"sum\"` super important.\n        autograd_grad_sample.add_hooks(model=self.module, loss_reduction=\"sum\")\n\n        # Override zero grad.\n        def dp_zero_grad(_self, *args, **kwargs):\n            _self.privacy_engine.zero_grad()\n\n        # Override step.\n        def dp_step(_self, **kwargs):\n            closure = kwargs.pop(\"closure\", None)\n\n            _self.privacy_engine.step(**kwargs)\n            _self.original_step(closure=closure)\n            _self.privacy_engine.unlock()  # Only enable creating new grads once parameters are updated.\n            _self.privacy_engine.steps += 1\n\n        def virtual_step(_self, **kwargs):\n            _self.privacy_engine.virtual_step(**kwargs)\n\n        def get_privacy_spent(_self, **kwargs):\n            return _self.privacy_engine.get_privacy_spent(**kwargs)\n\n        def get_training_stats(_self, **kwargs):\n            return _self.privacy_engine.get_training_stats(**kwargs)\n\n        optimizer.privacy_engine = self\n\n        optimizer.original_step = optimizer.step\n        optimizer.step = types.MethodType(dp_step, optimizer)\n\n        optimizer.original_zero_grad = optimizer.zero_grad\n        optimizer.zero_grad = types.MethodType(dp_zero_grad, optimizer)\n\n        optimizer.virtual_step = types.MethodType(virtual_step, optimizer)\n\n        # Make getting info easier.\n        optimizer.get_privacy_spent = types.MethodType(get_privacy_spent, optimizer)\n        optimizer.get_training_stats = types.MethodType(get_training_stats, optimizer)\n\n        self.module.privacy_engine = self\n\n        # Just to be safe, we also override `zero_grad` for module.\n        self.module.original_zero_grad = self.module.zero_grad\n        self.module.zero_grad = types.MethodType(dp_zero_grad, self.module)\n\n        # For easy detaching.\n        self.optimizer = optimizer\n\n    def detach(self):\n        optimizer = self.optimizer\n        optimizer.step = optimizer.original_step\n        optimizer.zero_grad = optimizer.original_zero_grad\n        delattr(optimizer, \"privacy_engine\")\n        delattr(optimizer, \"original_step\")\n        delattr(optimizer, \"original_zero_grad\")\n        delattr(optimizer, \"virtual_step\")\n        delattr(optimizer, \"get_privacy_spent\")\n        delattr(optimizer, \"get_training_stats\")\n\n        module = self.module\n        autograd_grad_sample.remove_hooks(module)\n        autograd_grad_sample.set_hooks_mode(\"default\")  # This is super important when there are multiple attaches!\n        module.zero_grad(skip_grad=True)  # noqa\n        module.zero_grad = module.original_zero_grad\n        delattr(module, \"original_zero_grad\")\n\n    @torch.no_grad()\n    def step(\n        self,\n        loss: torch.Tensor,\n        scale=1.,\n        # Function that takes in named_params and does something.\n        # This option was included to help with another spectrum analysis project.\n        callback: Optional[Callable] = None,\n    ):\n        if loss.dim() != 1:\n            raise ValueError(\n                f\"Expected `loss` to be the per-example loss 1-D tensor, but got a tensor with dims={loss.dim()}.\")\n\n        if self.clipping_mode == ClippingMode.ghost:\n            if callback is not None:\n                raise ValueError(\"Ghost clipping does not support `callback` in `optimizer.step`.\")\n            if scale != 1.:\n                raise ValueError(\"Ghost clipping does not support mixed-precision training.\")\n            self._ghost_step(loss=loss)\n        else:\n            self._step(loss=loss, scale=scale, callback=callback)\n\n    @torch.no_grad()\n    def virtual_step(self, loss: torch.Tensor, scale=1.):\n        \"\"\"Virtual step function when there's gradient accumulation.\"\"\"\n        if self.clipping_mode == ClippingMode.ghost:\n            self._ghost_virtual_step(loss=loss)\n        else:\n            self._virtual_step(loss=loss, scale=scale)\n\n    def zero_grad(self, skip_grad=False):\n        for name, param in self.named_params:\n            if hasattr(param, \"grad_sample\"):\n                del param.grad_sample\n            if hasattr(param, \"norm_sample\"):\n                del param.norm_sample\n            if hasattr(param, \"summed_grad\"):\n                del param.summed_grad\n            if not skip_grad:\n                if hasattr(param, \"grad\"):\n                    del param.grad\n\n    def _create_noisy_clipped_gradient(self):\n        \"\"\"Create noisy clipped gradient for `optimizer.step`.\n\n        Add noise and scale by inverse batch size.\n\n        Notes:\n            In ghost clipping, `summed_grad` stores previous micro-batches; `grad` stores current micro-batch.\n            In default clipping, `summed_grad` stores summed clipped gradients for all micro-batches.\n        \"\"\"\n\n        signals, noises = [], []\n        for name, param in self.named_params:\n            assert hasattr(param, 'summed_grad'), (\n                f\"Internal error: PrivacyEngine should not reach here; \"\n                f\"this means either \"\n                f\"1) there is parameter which requires gradient, but was not used in the computational graph, \"\n                f\"or 2) the backward hook registry failed to find the corresponding module to register.\"\n            )\n            param.grad = param.summed_grad  # Ultra important to override `.grad`.\n\n            if self.record_snr:\n                signals.append(param.grad.reshape(-1).norm(2))\n\n            if self.noise_multiplier > 0 and self.max_grad_norm > 0:\n                noise = torch.normal(\n                    mean=0,\n                    std=self.noise_multiplier * self.max_grad_norm,\n                    size=param.size(),\n                    device=param.device,\n                    dtype=param.dtype,\n                )\n                param.grad += noise\n                if self.record_snr:\n                    noises.append(noise.reshape(-1).norm(2))\n                del noise\n\n            param.grad /= self.batch_size\n\n        if self.record_snr and len(noises) > 0:\n            self.signal, self.noise = tuple(torch.stack(lst).norm(2).item() for lst in (signals, noises))\n            self.noise_limit = math.sqrt(self.num_params) * self.noise_multiplier * self.max_grad_norm\n            self.snr = self.signal / self.noise\n        else:\n            self.snr = math.inf  # Undefined!\n\n        self.lock()  # Make creating new gradients impossible, unless optimizer.step is called.\n\n    # --- ghost clipping ---\n    def _ghost_step(self, loss: torch.Tensor):\n        \"\"\"Run double-backward on per-example loss, then sum up all gradients and noise it.\"\"\"\n        if self._locked:  # Skip this gradient creation step if already created gradient and haven't stepped.\n            logging.warning(\"Attempted to step, but the engine is on lock.\")\n            return\n\n        self._ghost_virtual_step(loss)\n        self._create_noisy_clipped_gradient()\n\n    @torch.no_grad()\n    def _ghost_virtual_step(self, loss: torch.Tensor):\n        \"\"\"Backward twice to accumulate summed clipped gradients in `.summed_grad`.\n\n        We accumulate gradients in `.summed_grad` for micro-batching.\n        All of this copying actually creates a new 2x memory overhead.\n        \"\"\"\n        self._double_backward(loss)\n\n        for name, param in self.named_params:\n            if hasattr(param, 'summed_grad'):\n                param.summed_grad += param.grad\n            else:\n                param.summed_grad = param.grad\n\n            if hasattr(param, \"grad\"):\n                del param.grad\n            if hasattr(param, \"norm_sample\"):\n                del param.norm_sample\n            if hasattr(param, \"grad_sample\"):\n                del param.grad_sample\n\n    @torch.enable_grad()\n    def _double_backward(self, loss: torch.Tensor):\n        \"\"\"Given per-example losses, backward twice to accumulate summed clipped gradients in `.grad`.\"\"\"\n        first_loss = loss.sum()\n        first_loss.backward(retain_graph=True)\n\n        # Prepare for second backward.\n        autograd_grad_sample.set_hooks_mode(BackwardHookMode.ghost_grad)\n\n        # The first backward might have accumulated things we don't need into `.grad`;\n        # remove it before the second pass to avoid accumulating garbage.\n        for name, param in self.named_params:\n            if hasattr(param, \"grad\"):\n                del param.grad\n\n        coef_sample = self.get_coef_sample()\n        second_loss = (coef_sample * loss).sum(dim=0)\n        second_loss.backward()\n\n        # Prepare for first backward (in the next round).\n        autograd_grad_sample.set_hooks_mode(BackwardHookMode.ghost_norm)\n\n    def get_coef_sample(self) -> torch.Tensor:\n        \"\"\"Get per-example gradient scaling factor for clipping.\"\"\"\n        norm_sample = self.get_norm_sample()\n        return torch.clamp_max(self.max_grad_norm / (norm_sample + self.numerical_stability_constant), 1.)\n\n    def get_norm_sample(self) -> torch.Tensor:\n        \"\"\"Get per-example gradient norms.\"\"\"\n        norm_sample = torch.stack([param.norm_sample for name, param in self.named_params], dim=0).norm(2, dim=0)\n        return norm_sample\n\n    # --- default clipping ---\n    def _step(\n        self,\n        loss,\n        scale,\n        callback,\n    ):\n        \"\"\"Create noisy gradients.\n\n        Should be run right before you call `optimizer.step`.\n\n        This function does 3 things:\n            1) call `loss.backward()`\n            2) clip the current `.grad_sample` and add that to `.summed_grad`\n            3) noise the gradients\n        In mixed-precision training (with amp), the last two steps require knowing the loss scaling factor.\n\n        Args:\n            loss: The per-example loss; a 1-D tensor.\n            scale: The loss up-scaling factor in amp. In full precision, this arg isn't useful.\n        \"\"\"\n        if self._locked:  # Skip this gradient creation step if already created gradient and haven't stepped.\n            logging.warning(\"Attempted to step, but the engine is on lock.\")\n            return\n\n        norm_sample, coef_sample = self._accumulate_summed_grad(loss=loss, scale=scale)\n        # Collect stats for debugging.\n        self.max_clip = coef_sample.max().item()\n        self.min_clip = coef_sample.min().item()\n        self.med_clip = coef_sample.median().item()\n\n        if callback is not None:\n            callback(self)\n        self._create_noisy_clipped_gradient()\n\n    def _virtual_step(self, loss, scale):\n        self._accumulate_summed_grad(loss=loss, scale=scale)\n\n    @torch.no_grad()\n    def _accumulate_summed_grad(self, loss, scale):\n        \"\"\"Accumulate signal by summing clipped gradients.\n\n        Removes `.grad_sample` and `.grad` for each variable that requires grad at the end.\n        \"\"\"\n        with torch.enable_grad():\n            loss.sum(dim=0).backward()\n\n        norm_sample = []\n        for name, param in self.named_params:\n            try:\n                batch_size = param.grad_sample.size(0)\n            except AttributeError as error:\n                args = error.args\n                extra_msg = f\"\\n *** {name} parameter has no grad_sample attribute ***\"\n                error.args = (args[0] + extra_msg, *args[1:])\n                raise error\n            norm = param.grad_sample.reshape(batch_size, -1).norm(2, dim=1)\n            norm_sample.append(norm)\n\n        # The stack operation here is prone to error, thus clarify where the error is.\n        try:\n            norm_sample = torch.stack(norm_sample, dim=0).norm(2, dim=0)\n        except RuntimeError as runtime_error:\n            args = runtime_error.args\n\n            # Get the major shape.\n            shapes = collections.defaultdict(int)\n            for tensor in norm_sample:\n                shapes[tensor.size()] += 1\n\n            # Get the shape that most tensors have.\n            major_shape, major_count = max(shapes.items(), key=lambda x: x[1])\n\n            # Check which tensors don't have the major shape!\n            extra_msg = f\" \\n*** Major shape: {major_shape}\"\n            for (name, param), tensor in zip(list(self.named_params), norm_sample):\n                if tensor.size() != major_shape:\n                    extra_msg += f\", {name} wrong shape: {tensor.size()}\"\n            extra_msg += \" ***\"\n\n            runtime_error.args = (args[0] + extra_msg, *args[1:])\n            raise runtime_error\n\n        coef_sample = torch.clamp_max(\n            self.max_grad_norm * scale / (norm_sample + self.numerical_stability_constant), 1.\n        )\n        for name, param in self.named_params:\n            if not hasattr(param, 'summed_grad'):\n                param.summed_grad = 0.\n            current_device = param.grad_sample.device\n            param.summed_grad += torch.einsum(\"i,i...->...\", coef_sample.to(current_device), param.grad_sample)\n\n            # Aggressive memory saving -- delete everything except `.summed_grad` to save memory!\n            if hasattr(param, \"grad_sample\"):\n                # This must be deleted due to how `privacy_utils::supported_layers_grad_samplers.py` works!\n                #   When a parameter with `.grad_sample` is reused, the per-sample gradients are accumulated!\n                del param.grad_sample\n            if hasattr(param, \"grad\"):\n                del param.grad\n\n        return norm_sample, coef_sample\n\n    def get_privacy_spent(\n        self,\n        steps: Optional[int] = None,\n        accounting_mode: Optional[str] = None,\n        lenient=False\n    ) -> Dict:\n        if steps is None:\n            steps = self.steps\n        if accounting_mode is None:\n            accounting_mode = self.accounting_mode\n\n        privacy_results = {}  # Contains stats from all modes.\n        if accounting_mode in (AccountingMode.all_, AccountingMode.rdp):\n            try:\n                manager = accounting_manager.RDPManager(alphas=self.alphas)\n                privacy_results.update(\n                    manager.compute_epsilon(\n                        sigma=self.noise_multiplier,\n                        sample_rate=self.sample_rate,\n                        target_delta=self.target_delta,\n                        steps=steps,\n                    )\n                )\n            except Exception as err:\n                logging.fatal(\"RDP accounting failed! Double check privacy parameters.\")\n                if not lenient:\n                    raise err\n\n        if accounting_mode in (AccountingMode.all_, AccountingMode.glw):\n            try:\n                manager = accounting_manager.GLWManager(eps_error=self.eps_error)\n                privacy_results.update(\n                    manager.compute_epsilon(\n                        sigma=self.noise_multiplier,\n                        sample_rate=self.sample_rate,\n                        target_delta=self.target_delta,\n                        steps=steps\n                    )\n                )\n            except Exception as err:\n                logging.fatal(\n                    \"Numerical composition of tradeoff functions failed! Double check privacy parameters.\"\n                )\n                if not lenient:\n                    raise err\n\n        return privacy_results\n\n    def get_training_stats(self):\n        \"\"\"Get the clipping, signal, and noise statistics.\"\"\"\n        return {\n            \"med_clip\": self.med_clip,\n            \"max_clip\": self.max_clip,\n            \"min_clip\": self.min_clip,\n            \"snr\": self.snr,\n            \"signal\": self.signal,\n            \"noise\": self.noise,\n            \"noise_limit\": self.noise_limit,\n        }\n\n    def __repr__(self):\n        return (\n            f\"PrivacyEngine(\\n\"\n            f\"  target_epsilon={self.target_epsilon:.6f}, \\n\"\n            f\"  target_delta={self.target_delta:.6f}, \\n\"\n            f\"  noise_multiplier={self.noise_multiplier:.6f}, \\n\"\n            f\"  effective_noise_multiplier={self.effective_noise_multiplier:.6f}, \\n\"\n            f\"  epochs={self.epochs}, \\n\"\n            f\"  max_grad_norm={self.max_grad_norm}, \\n\"\n            f\"  sample_rate={self.sample_rate}, \\n\"\n            f\"  batch_size={self.batch_size}, \\n\"\n            f\"  accounting_mode={self.accounting_mode}, \\n\"\n            f\"  clipping_mode={self.clipping_mode}\\n\"\n            f\")\"\n        )\n",
    "Experiment Result": "The 'new linear scaling rule' involves conceptually combining learning rate (η) and number of steps (T) into a single hyperparameter r = η × T. The experimental setup facilitates this by providing configurable parameters for learning rate and epochs for individual training runs.\n\n- **Hyperparameter r (η × T) Configuration**: The `learning_rate` and `num_train_epochs` parameters are set independently. In `examples/classification/run_wrapper.py`, the `learning_rate` defaults to `5e-4` for non-private training, and `num_train_epochs` is derived from a base of `3` epochs, scaled by a factor dependent on the task (e.g., `factor=1` for SST-2 resulting in `3` epochs, `factor=2` for QNLI resulting in `6` epochs, and `factor=6` for MNLI/QQP resulting in `18` epochs).\n\n- **Optimal r(ε) Approximation Strategy**: The framework includes a `target_epsilon` parameter (defaulting to `8`). This allows researchers to perform 'cheap trials' with different `target_epsilon` values to find empirical `(ε, r(ε))` points, which can then be used to fit a first-order Taylor approximation for `r(ε)`.\n\n- **Differentially Private Gradient Descent (DP-GD) Settings**:\n    - **DP-GD Mechanism**: The core DP-GD is implemented using `private_transformers.PrivacyEngine`.\n    - **Batching**: The logical batch size (`batch_size` parameter in `PrivacyEngine`) is set to `1000`. This is achieved through gradient accumulation, with `per_device_train_batch_size=20` and `gradient_accumulation_steps=50` (`1000 / 20 = 50`). The `sample_size` for privacy accounting is derived from `len(train_dataset)`.\n    - **Clipping**: `per_example_max_grad_norm` is set to `0.1`, and `clipping_mode` is `default`.\n    - **Initialization**: The `randomly_initialize` flag (defaulting to `\"no\"` in `run_wrapper.py`) controls whether trainable parameters are re-initialized from scratch.\n    - **Optimizer**: `torch.optim.AdamW` is used with `adam_beta1=0.9` (standard momentum coefficient), `adam_epsilon=1e-08`, and `weight_decay=0`.\n    - **Learning Rate Schedule**: A linear decay schedule (`lr_decay=yes`) is applied.\n    - **Privacy Accounting**: The `accounting_mode` for the `PrivacyEngine` is set to `rdp` (Renyi Differential Privacy)."

    "Title": "Scalable First-Order Bayesian Optimization via Structured Automatic Differentiation",
    "Main Contributions": "The paper addresses the scalability issues of First-Order Bayesian Optimization (FOBO) in high dimensions, which typically involve expensive kernel matrix operations (O(n^2d^2) for multiplication, O(n^3d^3) for inversion) when incorporating gradient information. The main contributions include: 1) deriving analytical block-data-sparse structures for a large class of gradient kernel matrices, enabling an exact O(n^2d) matrix-vector multiply; 2) proposing an automatic differentiation (AD) framework that programmatically computes these data-sparse block structures for transformations and algebraic combinations of kernels; 3) deriving analogous structures for kernel matrices arising from Hessian observations, reducing complexity from O(n^2d^4) to O(n^2d^2); 4) demonstrating improved scaling through numerical experiments; and 5) applying these methods to Bayesian Optimization, showing enhanced performance on test functions.",
    "Methodology": "The core methodology involves exploiting structured properties of kernel matrices when derivative information is incorporated. This is achieved by: 1) categorizing kernels based on input types (isotropic, stationary linear functional, dot product) and deriving explicit G-operator forms; 2) applying a chain rule for composite kernels (k = f o g), showing G[f o g] is a rank-one correction to G[g]; 3) defining rules for sums, products, direct sums, direct products, and vertical rescaling of kernels, leading to low-rank corrections or diagonal structures that maintain computational efficiency; 4) generalizing these structures for warped kernels by factoring out Jacobian terms, reducing MVM complexity to O(n^2d); and 5) extending these principles to composite kernels like neural network, RBF-network, and spectral mixture kernels. For Hessian observations, a structured representation of the Hessian-Hessian covariance is derived, reducing MVM to O(n^2d^2). The approach is implemented in CovarianceFunctions.jl, a Julia library, which uses user-defined types to represent computational graphs of kernels, leverages ForwardDiff.jl for derivatives, and employs lazy evaluation for O(1) memory complexity. For Bayesian Optimization, a specific kernel (x·y+c)^2 combined with a Matérn-5/2 kernel is used to model objectives as a sum of a quadratic function and a quickly varying non-convex function, with Expected Improvement as the acquisition function optimized via L-BFGS with random restarts.",
    "Experimental Setup": "The experimental setup includes: 1) **Scaling on Synthetic Data:** Benchmarks used the non-separable rational quadratic kernel, exponentiated dot-product, and neural network kernels to study practical scaling with respect to dimension and number of observations. Scaling plots were generated with a single thread, while heat-maps (showing execution time in n-d space) were run with 24 threads on 12 cores. Comparisons were made between the naive (O(d^2) for gradient, O(d^4) for Hessian) and fast (O(d) for gradient, O(d^2) for Hessian) MVM algorithms. 2) **Comparison to Prior Work:** Performance was compared against popular libraries like GPyTorch, GPFlow, and Scikit-Learn (most lack efficient gradient inference) and D-SKIP (Eriksson et al., 2018), an approximate method for separable product kernels. This comparison included MVM times and accuracy using RBF gradient kernel matrices. 3) **Bayesian Optimization:** Optimization algorithms were benchmarked on three non-convex test functions: Griewank, Ackley, and Rastrigin, in 4D and 16D. Input domains were scaled to [-1,1]^d, outputs to [0,1], and global optima shifted to 1d/4. Experiments involved 128 independent runs, reporting the average optimality gap for strategies including random sampling, L-BFGS (with and without random restarts), standard BO, BO with a quadratic mixture kernel (BO-Q), FOBO, and FOBO with a quadratic mixture kernel (FOBO-Q). All BO variants used the expected improvement acquisition function, numerically optimized with L-BFGS.",
    "Limitations": "The paper acknowledges several limitations: 1) the global optimization of non-convex functions remains NP-hard, and thus the proposed methods cannot guarantee a general solution, as exemplified by the difficulty in optimizing the 16D Ackley function; 2) while structures for Hessian observations were derived and their computational feasibility demonstrated, they were not used for Bayesian Optimization experiments in this work; and 3) a comprehensive comparison of first and second-order Bayesian Optimization is deferred to future work, with the current focus being on enabling such investigations.",
    "Future Research Directions": "The authors suggest several future research avenues: 1) linking their structured AD technology with algorithms for computing hyper-parameter gradients (e.g., Maclaurin et al., 2015) to enable efficient FOBO of hyper-parameters; 2) developing analysis-based fast algorithms, such as the Fast Kernel Transform (Ryan et al., 2022), for gradient kernels to mitigate the O(n^2) complexity for very large numbers of observations (n), particularly in low dimensions; 3) exploiting redundant information in BO trajectories, especially when converging to a minimum, using sparse linear solvers (e.g., Ament and Gomes, 2021); and 4) exploring the application of Hessian observations for Bayesian Quadrature, given the established benefits of second-order information for integral estimation.",
