    "Title": "Scalable First-Order Bayesian Optimization via Structured Automatic Differentiation",
    "Main Contributions": "The paper addresses the scalability issues of First-Order Bayesian Optimization (FOBO) in high dimensions, particularly when incorporating gradient and Hessian information into Gaussian process (GP) surrogate models. Naïve approaches lead to prohibitive O(n^2d^2) and O(n^3d^3) complexities for gradient observations, and even higher for Hessian. The main contributions include: 1) Deriving analytical block-data-sparse structures for a large class of gradient kernel matrices, achieving an O(n^2d) matrix-vector multiply. 2) Proposing an Automatic Differentiation (AD) framework that programmatically computes these data-sparse block structures for various kernel transformations and combinations, with an open-source implementation. 3) Deriving analogous structures for Hessian kernel matrices, reducing the complexity from O(n^2d^4) to O(n^2d^2) for matrix-vector multiplication. 4) Providing numerical experiments demonstrating the improved scaling and applicability of these methods. 5) Comparing the proposed techniques against existing libraries and methods. 6) Applying these scalable methods to Bayesian Optimization, showing improved performance on benchmark functions.",
    "Methodology": "The core methodology involves exploiting structured automatic differentiation (AD) to uncover and leverage data-sparse structures within kernel matrices when conditioned on gradient or Hessian information. For gradient kernels, the paper observes that the matrix-valued operator G = ∇x∇⊤y acting on various kernel functions k(x,y) (isotropic, stationary linear functional, dot product, and their combinations/transformations) yields highly structured blocks. A chain rule is derived (G[f ◦ g] is a rank-one correction to G[g]) which is extended to sums, products, direct sums/products (separable kernels), vertical rescaling, and warping functions. This allows for an O(d) matrix-vector multiply with the G[k](xi,xj) blocks. For Hessian kernels, analogous structured representations are derived for isotropic kernels, enabling O(d^2) matrix-vector multiplies for the Hessian-Hessian covariance. The approach is implemented in `CovarianceFunctions.jl` in Julia, using `ForwardDiff.jl` for regular derivatives and employing lazy evaluation to achieve O(1) memory complexity for matrix-vector multiplication. For Bayesian Optimization, a composite kernel combining a quadratic component (x·y+c)^2 and a Matérn-5/2 kernel is used, with the Expected Improvement (EI) acquisition function optimized via L-BFGS with random restarts.",
    "Experimental Setup": "The experimental setup includes: 1) **Scaling on Synthetic Data**: Matrix-vector multiplication benchmarks using non-separable rational quadratic, exponentiated dot-product, and neural network kernels. Runtimes are measured as functions of dimensions (d) and number of observations (n), exhibiting O(d) scaling for gradient kernels and O(d^2) for Hessian kernels (when n=1). Heatmaps illustrate sub-second runtimes across a wider n-d space, leveraging parallel processing (24 threads on 12 cores). 2) **Comparison to Prior Work**: Performance and complexity are compared against popular GP libraries (GPyTorch, GPFlow, Scikit-Learn) and D-SKIP (Eriksson et al., 2018), an approximate method for scaling GPs with derivatives. The proposed method demonstrates exactness (machine precision accuracy) and often superior performance compared to D-SKIP, especially when considering D-SKIP's pre-processing costs. 3) **Bayesian Optimization Experiments**: Optimization algorithms are benchmarked on non-convex test functions: Griewank, Ackley, and Rastrigin (from Bingham and Surjanovic, 2013). Input domains are scaled to [-1,1]^d, outputs to [0,1], and global optima shifted. Strategies compared include random sampling, L-BFGS (with and without restarts), standard BO, BO with the proposed quadratic mixture kernel (BO-Q), FOBO, and FOBO with the quadratic mixture kernel (FOBO-Q). Experiments are run in 4D and 16D, with average optimality gaps over 128 independent runs.",
    "Limitations": "The global optimization of non-convex functions remains NP-hard, and thus the proposed methods cannot universally solve this problem. For instance, the optimum of the 16D Ackley function proved elusive for all tested methods, likely due to its rapidly shrinking domain of attraction in high dimensions. While structured representations for Hessian observations were derived and their computational feasibility demonstrated, they were not explicitly used for Bayesian Optimization within this paper, with a comprehensive comparison of first and second-order BO being deferred to future work. The O(n^2) complexity, even with linear scaling in d, can still become a bottleneck for a very large number of observations (n).",
    "Future Research Directions": "Future research directions include: 1) Linking the proposed structured AD technology with existing algorithms for computing hyper-parameter gradients (e.g., Maclaurin et al., 2015) to enable efficient FOBO of hyper-parameters. 2) Investigating analysis-based fast algorithms, such as the Fast Kernel Transform, for gradient kernels to overcome the O(n^2) complexity bottleneck for very large numbers of observations, particularly in low dimensions. 3) Exploiting redundant information in BO trajectories, especially when the optimization converges to a minimum, potentially using sparse linear solvers to further improve efficiency. 4) Exploring the application of Hessian observations, which were derived and shown to be computationally feasible, to Bayesian Quadrature, given the established benefits of second-order information for integral estimation (e.g., Laplace approximation).",
    "Experiment Code": null,

    "Title": "Efficient Nonmyopic Bayesian Optimization via One-Shot Multi-Step Trees",
    "Main Contributions": "The paper provides the first efficient implementation of general multi-step lookahead Bayesian Optimization (BO). Combined with efficient multi-step Gaussian Process (GP) fantasization, the approach makes multi-step expected improvement computationally tractable and demonstrates superior optimization performance over existing myopic and nonmyopic methods on a wide range of benchmarks.",
    "Methodology": "The core methodology involves formulating multi-step lookahead as a sequence of nested optimization problems within a multi-step scenario tree. To address intractability, the paper proposes a \"one-shot\" approach where all decision variables across the tree are optimized jointly as a single, higher-dimensional problem, enabled by a re-parameterization trick that expresses fantasy samples as deterministic functions of base samples and decision variables. This allows for fully differentiable acquisition functions, optimized using gradient-based methods like L-BFGS-B with random restarting and warm-start initialization. A key technical enabler is \"multi-step fast fantasies,\" which uses efficient linear algebra insights and batched computation on parallelizable hardware (e.g., GPUs) for recursively sampling and conditioning GP models, including a novel method for updating GPyTorch's LOVE caches. Special, more scalable instances are also explored: \"Multi-Step Path\" (linear growth in horizon by limiting subtree branches to a single path) and \"Non-Adaptive Approximation (ENO)\" (linear growth by approximating future adaptive decisions with non-adaptive ones using a one-step batch value function).",
    "Experimental Setup": "The algorithms were implemented in BoTorch and GPyTorch, utilizing a Gaussian Process (GP) with a constant mean and a Matérn 5/2 ARD kernel. GP hyperparameters were re-estimated by maximizing the evidence after each iteration. Experiments started with 2d random initial observations and ran for 20d iterations, with 100 repeats per function and method. Performance was measured using GAP = (yi - y0) / (y* - y0). Experiments were conducted on CPU Linux machines, using one core per experiment. Benchmarks included nine 'hard' synthetic functions (e.g., eggholder, dropwave, shubert, rastrigin4, ackley2, ackley5, bukin, shekel5, shekel7) and seven real-world hyperparameter tuning functions (SVM, LDA, logistic regression, neural network tuning on Boston Housing and Breast Cancer datasets, and robot pushing 3d/4d). Baselines for comparison included Expected Improvement (EI), BINOCULARS (12.EI.s variant), and the 'envelope two-step' (ETS) method. Specific configurations for the proposed methods included 'k-step' (k=2,3,4) with varying Gauss-Hermite samples (m1=10, m2=5, m3=3), 'k-path' (multi-step path variant with one sample per stage), and 'k-ENO' (non-adaptive approximation).",
    "Limitations": "The general one-shot optimization problem has a dimensionality that grows exponentially with the lookahead horizon (k), practically limiting the number of lookahead steps (e.g., less than 5). Experimental results show diminishing returns beyond 3-step lookahead, suggesting potential challenges in optimizing increasingly complex objectives or an increased reliance on model accuracy which may not hold in practice. The paper notes that a baseline method (ETS) relies on the unrealistic assumption of global optima for second-stage value functions, which can lead to objective discontinuities, highlighting a challenge that their method aims to overcome.",

    "Title": "Accelerating Look-ahead in Bayesian Optimization: Multilevel Monte Carlo is All you Need",
    "Main Contributions": "The paper addresses the computational complexity of approximating multi-step look-ahead acquisition functions in Bayesian Optimization (BO), which increases exponentially with the number of look-ahead steps for current methods. The main contribution is the application of Multilevel Monte Carlo (MLMC) to these problems, improving the computational complexity. For two- and three-step look-ahead acquisition functions involving nested expectations and maximizations, MLMC is shown to achieve the canonical Monte Carlo (MC) convergence rate (O(ε^-2) for MSE ε^2), significantly outperforming naive nested MC which degrades to O(ε^-4) for two nested operations. The theoretical findings are numerically verified on several benchmark examples, demonstrating that MLMC can achieve the same accuracy with less computational cost or higher accuracy for a given budget, particularly when high accuracy is required.",
    "Methodology": "The core methodology involves leveraging Multilevel Monte Carlo (MLMC) to efficiently approximate analytically intractable look-ahead acquisition functions in BO. Multi-step acquisition functions are formulated as Markov Decision Processes (MDPs) and optimized using nested Sample Average Approximation (SAA) and Monte Carlo (MC) estimations. MLMC works by constructing a telescoping sum of estimators with increasing accuracy and cost across levels (e.g., Ml = 2^l samples), performing most simulations with low accuracy/cost and fewer with high accuracy/cost. The error of SAA with nested MC is decomposed into \"variance\" and \"bias\" terms, allowing MLMC application. Two MLMC formulations are discussed: a multilevel estimator for the maximizer (x*ML) and an MLMC approximation of the acquisition function (αML(x)). An antithetic coupling approach is introduced for the maximizer estimator to further improve convergence rates, potentially achieving canonical O(ε^-2) complexity. Gaussian Processes (GPs) are used as surrogate models for the black-box functions.",
    "Experimental Setup": "The numerical tests were conducted on various synthetic functions, including a 1D toy example (g(x) = e^-(x-2)^2 + e^-(x-6)^2/10 + 1/(x^2 + 1)), Ackley (d=2), DropWave (d=2), Branin (d=2), Hartmann6 (d=6), and Cosine8 (d=8) from BoTorch. The error metric used for the overall BO algorithm is Normalized Mean Squared Error (NMSE), defined as E[||max_Dk g(x) - g(x*)||^2 / ||max_D0 g(x) - g(x*)||^2], which measures relative improvement over initial values and normalizes to [0,1]. Experiments were typically run with 20 or 40 realizations, randomly initialized with 2*d observations. The Matérn kernel was applied for Gaussian Process regression. The performance of 2-step look-ahead acquisition functions (1-EI + 2-EI or 2-EI + 2-EI) computed using MLMC was compared against standard MC for the same epsilon (e.g., ε = 0.2 or 0.15). L-BFGS with multi-start initialization was used for local optimization. Numerical verification of inner/outer MC rates, Corollary 1 and 2 complexities, and MLMC variance assumptions (β=1 and β≈1.5 with antithetic coupling) was performed.",
    "Limitations": "The theoretical study mainly focuses on two- and three-step look-ahead settings, although the approach is generalizable. For the multilevel estimator of the maximizer, strong assumptions were made, and the complete, more subtle, and challenging theory is deferred to future work, particularly regarding the unbiased nature of maximizer increments. Assumption 3, which implies that the strong rate of convergence of acquisition function increments leads to a corresponding rate for the maximizer increments, is stated but its proof is deferred. MLMC may not be able to achieve the canonical rate with an increasing number of look-ahead steps due to the curse of dimensionality, which can violate regularity assumptions. In practice, MLMC requires careful tuning to balance 'variance' and 'bias' for optimal performance, including selecting the finest level and number of samples, which can be challenging to compute interactively. The matching strategies for optimizers (e.g., point matching or backward matching) involve trade-offs between robustness and computational cost. The multilevel value function estimation (Corollary 1) showed worse performance than MC in BO, despite theoretical advantages, which was attributed to higher computational cost.",
    "Future Research Directions": "Future research directions include further theoretical analysis of the MLMC approach, particularly for the general n-nested case using Multi-Index Monte Carlo (MIMC) to handle the curse of dimensionality under mixed regularity assumptions. Investigating Randomized MLMC and MIMC is suggested to eliminate bias and reduce costly tuning, potentially at the cost of a larger constant. Exploring the introduction of multilevel quasi-Monte Carlo (MLQMC) or multi-index quasi-Monte Carlo (MIQMC) methods to achieve even faster convergence rates (e.g., O(ε^-1)) for smooth integrands, while acknowledging that performance degenerates to standard MC rates for non-smooth cases. Applying the improved multi-step look-ahead methods to constrained optimization problems is also noted as a promising area. An exhaustive exploration of the ideal acquisition function within this framework is left for future work, with logEI family mentioned as compelling candidates.",
    "Experiment Code": "import time\nfrom typing import Any, Callable, Dict, List, Optional, Type, Union\n\nimport torch\nfrom botorch.acquisition import (\n    AcquisitionFunction,\n        inner_sampler=inner_samplers[1],\n        arg_fac=valfunc_argfacs[1],\n    )\n    running_val = running_val + stage_val\n\n    # construct fantasy points\n    base_samples = sampler1.base_samples = base_samples1\n    sampler2 = IIDNormalSampler(sample_shape=torch.Size([len(base_samples2)]))\n    sampler2.base_samples = base_samples2\n\n    fantasy_model1 = model.fantasize(\n        X=Xs[1], sampler=sampler1, observation_noise=True, propagate_grads=True\n    )\n    fantasy_model2 = model.fantasize(\n        X=Xs[1], sampler=sampler2, observation_noise=True, propagate_grads=True\n    )\n\n    # augment sample weights appropriately\n    sample_weights = _construct_sample_weights(\n        prev_weights=sample_weights, sampler=samplers[1]\n    )\n\n    # compute second step\n    stage_val1 = _compute_stage_value(\n        model=fantasy_model1,\n        valfunc_cls=valfunc_cls[2],\n        X=Xs[2][0::2],\n        objective=objective,\n        posterior_transform=posterior_transform,\n        inner_sampler=inner_samplers[2],\n        arg_fac=valfunc_argfacs[2],\n    )\n\n    stage_val2 = _compute_stage_value(\n        model=fantasy_model2,\n        valfunc_cls=valfunc_cls[2],\n        X=Xs[2][1::2],\n        objective=objective,\n        posterior_transform=posterior_transform,\n        inner_sampler=inner_samplers[2],\n        arg_fac=valfunc_argfacs[2],\n    )\n\n    running_val = running_val + 1/2 * torch.cat((stage_val1, stage_val2), dim=0)\n\n    batch_shape = running_val.shape[2:]\n    # expand sample weights to make sure it is the same shape as running_val,\n    # because we need to take a sum over sample weights for computing the\n    # weighted average\n    sample_weights = sample_weights.expand(running_val.shape)\n    return (running_val * sample_weights).view(-1, *batch_shape).sum(dim=0)\n\n\ndef dist_matrix(x, y):\n    # compute the distance matrix of two vectors\n    xsq = torch.sum(x ** 2, dim=(1, 2))  # (N,)\n    ysq = torch.sum(y ** 2, dim=(1, 2))  # (M,)\n\n    mixprod = -2 * x.view(x.shape[0], -1) @ y.view(y.shape[0], -1).T  # (N, M)\n\n    return torch.sqrt(xsq.unsqueeze(1) + mixprod + ysq.unsqueeze(0))  # (N,1)+(N,M)+(1,M) => (N, M)\n\nimport torch\nimport numpy as np\n\n\ndef optimize_mlmc(inc_function, eps, dl, np.arange(dl, L + 1)\n\n    Ncon = (np.sum(np.sqrt(varc * 2 ** (-levels * (beta - gamma)))) + np.sqrt(var0 * 2 ** gamma)) / (eps ** 2)\n    # number of outer samples at each level\n    Nl = np.ceil(np.maximum(Ncon * np.sqrt(varc * 2 ** (-levels * (beta + gamma))), 2)).astype(int)\n\n    # TODO: Check one-shot ml f is the same as single f(z^*)\n    # z is the optimizer and f is the 'point':\n                f += fl\n                z += zl\n            else:\n                if level == (levels - dl)[0]:\n                    f = fl\n                    z = zl\n     "Title": "A General Framework for User-Guided Bayesian Optimization",
    "Main Contributions": "The paper introduces ColaBO, a novel Bayesian-principled framework for user-guided Bayesian Optimization (BO). The main problem addressed is the limited ability of traditional BO to effectively incorporate diverse prior knowledge or beliefs from domain experts beyond standard kernel hyperparameters. ColaBO allows practitioners to inject prior beliefs about function properties, such as the likely location of the optimizer, the optimal value, or preference relations, directly into the Gaussian Process surrogate model. Key findings include that ColaBO substantially accelerates optimization when the prior information is accurate and retains approximately default performance even when the prior is misleading, demonstrating robustness superior to some existing methods like πBO. The framework is generally applicable across different Monte Carlo acquisition functions.",
    "Methodology": "ColaBO's methodology involves reshaping the Gaussian Process (GP) prior to reflect user-defined beliefs, diverging from the typical assumption of Gaussian posteriors. It introduces a 'user belief over functions' (ρ(f)) which acts as a likelihood, weighting functions based on their alignment with the user's prior. This creates a belief-weighted prior p(f|ρ). Upon observing data D, the posterior p(f|D, ρ) is formed by proportionally weighting the standard posterior p(f|D) with ρ(f). The framework leverages decoupled posterior sampling (Matheron's rule) where ρ affects the draw from the prior and observations affect a deterministic update. Due to the resulting non-Gaussian posteriors, ColaBO employs Monte Carlo (MC) acquisition functions. It adapts standard MC-Expected Improvement (MC-EI) and MC-Max-Value Entropy Search (MC-MES) by weighting the sampled functions with their probability under the user belief ρ(f). This ensures that the acquisition function prioritizes regions aligned with the user's knowledge. The process involves sampling functions from the prior, evaluating them against the user's belief, updating them pathwise with observed data, and then maximizing the belief-weighted MC acquisition function.",
    "Experimental Setup": "ColaBO was evaluated on synthetic functions and real-world hyperparameter optimization (HPO) tasks. For synthetic tasks, the study used Hartmann (3D, 4D, 6D), Levy (5D), Rosenbrock (6D), and Stybtang (7D) functions, with varying levels of synthetic noise. Priors over the optimal location were generated as 'well-located' (10% offset from optimum) and 'poorly located' (maximally offset, mode moved to search space edge) Gaussian distributions. Experiments also included priors over the optimal value. For real-world HPO, ColaBO was tested on five 6D deep learning tasks from LCBench and three 4D tasks from PD1, utilizing priors provided by MF-Prior-Bench. ColaBO variants (ColaBO-LogEI and ColaBO-MES) were benchmarked against vanilla LogEI, MES, πBO, and decoupled Thompson sampling. All methods were implemented in BoTorch using a squared exponential kernel (with Matérn-5/2 kernel results in the appendix). Hyperparameters were estimated via MAP at each iteration. Initialization typically involved the prior's mode plus 2 Sobol samples, or just the mode for HPO tasks. Approximation quality was assessed by comparing the arg max of ColaBO-LogEI to vanilla LogEI on Hartmann (3D). MC approximation budgets (number of function samples, RFFs, resamples, optimal values for MES) were specified for each task type.",
    "Limitations": "ColaBO's primary limitation is its computational expense, driven by the reliance on multiple steps of Monte Carlo integration, which can require tens of seconds per evaluation for desired accuracy. The process of obtaining draws from priors over the optimum (ρ_x*) scales exponentially with the dimensionality of the prior, potentially making it impractical for high-dimensional user beliefs. Furthermore, the framework cannot fully utilize Quasi-Monte Carlo methods for draws from the predictive posterior (only for RFF weights) and cannot fix base samples for ensuring acquisition function consistency across the search space. The Random Fourier Feature (RFF) approximation used for function draws introduces bias, which is more pronounced with the Matérn 5/2 kernel compared to the squared exponential kernel, suggesting a preference for the latter.",
    "Future Research Directions": "Future research directions for ColaBO include exploring more accurate and efficient sampling procedures for the belief-weighted prior, potentially drawing inspiration from recent advancements in stochastic gradient descent-based sampling. Another avenue is to investigate variational inference or pre-trained model approaches to develop a representative belief-biased model with an analytical posterior. This could significantly reduce the runtime of ColaBO and broaden its applicability. Additionally, applying ColaBO to multi-fidelity optimization is suggested as a promising direction to further enhance efficiency, especially for computationally intensive tasks like deep learning pipeline optimization.",

    "Title": "Multi-Fidelity Bayesian Optimization via Deep Neural Networks",
    "Main Contributions": "The paper proposes Deep Neural Network Multi-Fidelity Bayesian Optimization (DNN-MFBO) to address the inefficiency and inaccuracy of existing multi-fidelity BO methods. These prior methods either ignore or oversimplify the strong, complex correlations between different fidelities. DNN-MFBO uses a stacked deep neural network model to flexibly capture complex (potentially nonlinear and nonstationary) relationships across fidelities, thereby improving objective function estimation. It also introduces a tractable and efficient method for calculating a mutual information based acquisition function using sequential, fidelity-wise Gauss-Hermite quadrature and moment-matching. The method demonstrates superior performance in optimizing objective functions with smaller query costs on synthetic benchmarks and real-world engineering design applications.",
    "Methodology": "DNN-MFBO utilizes deep neural networks (DNNs) as a probabilistic surrogate model. For multi-fidelity modeling, it stacks NNs where each NN models one fidelity. For fidelity 'm > 1', the NN input includes the original input 'x' and the output from the previous fidelity 'f_{m-1}(x)', capturing complex relationships through 'f_m(x) = h(x, f_{m-1}(x))'. For 'm=1', the input is just 'x'. Bayesian inference treats NN weights in the output layer as random variables with a standard normal prior and other weights as hyperparameters. A stochastic variational learning algorithm, leveraging the reparameterization trick, jointly estimates the Gaussian posterior for the random weights and other hyperparameters by maximizing an Evidence Lower Bound (ELBO). For optimization, a mutual information based acquisition function, 'a(x, m) = (1/λm) * I(f*, f_m(x)|D)', is used. Output posteriors 'p(f_m(x)|D)' are approximated as Gaussian via fidelity-wise moment matching and Gauss-Hermite quadrature. The conditional entropy term of the acquisition function is computed using a Monte-Carlo approximation for function maximums 'f*', further employing sequential Gauss-Hermite quadrature and moment matching to approximate conditional posteriors. The acquisition function is optimized using L-BFGS with random initialization.",
    "Experimental Setup": "The method was evaluated on three synthetic benchmark tasks: Branin function (2D input, 3 fidelities), Park1 function (4D input, 2 fidelities), and Levy function (2D input, 3 fidelities), which feature nonlinear and nonstationary transformations between fidelities. Real-world applications included: 1) Mechanical Plate Vibration Design, optimizing three material properties (Young's modulus, Poisson's ratio, mass density) for a 3D plate's fourth vibration mode frequency, with two fidelities (coarse vs. dense mesh). 2) Thermal Conductor Design, optimizing three parameters (x-radius, y-radius, angle) of an elliptical hole to minimize heat conduction time, also with two fidelities (coarse vs. dense mesh). Competing methods included Multi-Fidelity Sequential Kriging (MF-SKO), MF-GP-UCB, Multi-Fidelity Predictive Entropy Search (MF-PES), Multi-Fidelity Maximum Entropy Search (MF-MES), Multi-task NN based BO (MTNN-BO), and Single Fidelity MES (SF-MES). DNN-MFBO and MTNN-BO were implemented with TensorFlow using ReLU activation. Hyperparameters (NN depth, width, learning rate) were tuned using SMAC3. Initial training points varied by benchmark (e.g., Branin/Levy: 20, 20, 2 samples for fidelities 1, 2, 3 respectively; Park1: 5, 2 samples; real-world: 20 low, 5 high). Query costs (λm) were set, e.g., (1, 10, 100) for 3 fidelities. Performance was measured by simple regret (SR), inference regret (IR) for synthetic tasks, and best queried function values for real-world tasks, along with average query time. Experiments were repeated five times on a Linux workstation with a 16-core Intel Xeon CPU and 16GB RAM.",
    "Limitations": "The existing multi-fidelity Bayesian Optimization methods either ignore or oversimplify strong, complex correlations between different fidelities, leading to limited accuracy and efficiency. The proposed DNN-MFBO primarily focuses on discrete fidelities, indicating that its direct applicability to continuous fidelities is not addressed in this work. While the acquisition function calculation in DNN-MFBO is computationally tractable, it is described as 'quite complex' and relies on analytical approximations (e.g., Monte-Carlo, moment matching, Gauss-Hermite quadrature), which inherently introduce some degree of approximation error or simplification compared to exact posteriors.",
    "Future Research Directions": "Not mentioned",

    "Experiment Result": "The experimental setup involves a robotic pushing task simulated using the Box2D physics engine. The objective is to find optimal robot parameters to minimize the Euclidean distance between the final position(s) of the pushed object(s) and a specified goal position(s).\n\n**Multi-fidelity Aspect:** The parameter `simu_steps` (simulation steps) is crucial for multi-fidelity optimization. It determines the duration and therefore the fidelity of the simulation. A smaller `simu_steps` value would correspond to a lower fidelity (faster, less accurate), while a larger value would represent a higher fidelity (slower, more accurate).\n\n**Input Parameters (Design Variables `x`):** The simulation accepts various command-line arguments as input parameters, which DNN-MFBO would optimize:\n*   `rx`, `ry`: Initial X and Y coordinates of the robot hand.\n*   `gx`, `gy`: Goal X and Y coordinates for the object(s).\n*   `init_angle`: Initial angle of the robot hand.\n*   `xvel`, `yvel`: X and Y components of the pushing velocity.\n*   `rtor`: Torque applied by the robot.\n*   For multi-robot/multi-object scenarios (e.g., `generate_simudata_2robot2thing.py`), parameters are duplicated for each robot and object (e.g., `rx2`, `ry2`, `xvel2`, `yvel2`, `simu_steps2`, `init_angle2`, `rtor2`, `gx2`, `gy2`).\n\n**Simulation Settings:**\n*   **Physics Engine:** Box2D (pybox2d).\n*   **Object Properties:** Objects are typically `circle` or `rectangle` shapes, with specified `size`, `friction` (e.01), and `density` (e.g., 0.05). The robot hand is usually a `rectangle`.\n*   **World Dimensions:** A 500x500 unit world with a static base.\n*   **Time Stepping:** Fixed `TARGET_FPS = 100`, `TIME_STEP = 1.0/TARGET_FPS`, with `VEL_ITERS=10`, `POS_ITERS=10` for physics resolution.\n*   **Pushing Mechanism:** The robot applies a 'wrench' (force and/or torque) to move objects. For `simu_push`, the desired velocity is directed from the robot to the object. For `simu_push2` and `simu_push_2robot2thing`, explicit `xvel`/`yvel` and `rtor` can be specified.\n\n**Output (`f_m(x)`):** The return value for each simulation run is a scalar representing the total Euclidean distance(s) from the goal position(s), which DNN-MFBO aims to minimize. For multi-object tasks, this is the sum of distances for all objects."

    "Title": "Efficient Hyperparameter Optimization with Adaptive Fidelity Identification",
    "Main Contributions": "FastBO proposes a multi-fidelity Bayesian Optimization (BO) method that adaptively determines the appropriate fidelity for each hyperparameter configuration to fit the surrogate model. This is achieved through the novel concepts of \"efficient point\" and \"saturation point.\" The method efficiently provides strong performance and offers a general strategy to extend any single-fidelity method to a multi-fidelity setting, highlighting its generality and applicability in HPO and NAS.",
    "Methodology": "FastBO extends Bayesian Optimization by introducing adaptive fidelity identification. It defines an \"efficient point\" for each configuration as the resource level beyond which doubling resources yields diminishing performance improvement; this point is used to fit the surrogate model. A \"saturation point\" is defined as the resource level where performance stabilizes, indicating approximate final fidelity. The process involves a warm-up stage for initial observations and early termination of poor configurations, followed by learning curve estimation. Efficient and saturation points are then adaptively extracted. Configurations are evaluated up to their efficient point to update the surrogate model, and a post-processing stage evaluates a select few promising configurations to their saturation points to identify the optimal one. This adaptive fidelity identification strategy can also generalize to extend other single-fidelity methods to multi-fidelity settings.",
    "Experimental Setup": "FastBO was evaluated and compared against several established multi-fidelity HPO and NAS methods, including Random Search (RS), standard BO, ASHA, Hyperband, PASHA, A-BOHB, A-CQR, BOHB, DyHPO, and Hyper-Tune. The experiments were conducted on three recognized benchmarks: LCBench, NAS-Bench-201, and FCNet. Performance was primarily assessed based on \"anytime performance,\" demonstrating its ability to gain an advantage early and rapidly converge to the global optimum.",
    "Limitations": "The paper implicitly suggests that the current applicability and scalability of FastBO could be improved, particularly when dealing with larger search spaces and distributed computing systems. The paper serves as an extended abstract, omitting finer details, which might limit a comprehensive understanding of specific implementation aspects or theoretical nuances.",
    "Future Research Directions": "Future work should focus on refining and expanding FastBO to effectively handle larger search spaces and integrate with distributed computing systems. This would aim to significantly improve its applicability and overall scalability for more complex and resource-intensive hyperparameter optimization and neural architecture search tasks.",

    "Title": "Batch Multi-Fidelity Bayesian Optimization with  Deep Auto-Regressive Networks",
    "Main Contributions": "The paper introduces Batch Multi-Fidelity Bayesian Optimization with Deep Auto-Regressive Networks (BMBO-DARN) to optimize expensive, black-box functions by effectively leveraging multi-fidelity evaluations. Its main contributions are: 1) A deep auto-regressive model using Bayesian Neural Networks (BNNs) to capture complex, non-linear, and non-stationary relationships across all fidelities, improving surrogate learning. 2) A novel batch acquisition function based on the Max-value Entropy Search (MES) principle that explicitly penalizes highly correlated queries to promote diversity and balance benefit-cost. 3) An efficient alternating optimization algorithm for maximizing the batch acquisition function, avoiding the computationally expensive combinatorial search over discrete fidelities and guaranteeing improvement at each step.",
    "Methodology": "BMBO-DARN employs a deep auto-regressive model where a chain of Bayesian Neural Networks (BNNs) models each fidelity. The input to the BNN for a given fidelity m consists of the original input x and the outputs from all previous fidelities f1(x), ..., fm-1(x), enabling the capture of complex cross-fidelity relationships. Hamiltonian Monte Carlo (HMC) sampling is used for robust posterior inference of NN weights. For batch querying, a batch acquisition function derived from Max-value Entropy Search (MES) is proposed, which incorporates a penalty for correlated queries and normalizes by total query cost. To efficiently compute this function, posterior samples of NN weights are used with moment matching to approximate the joint posterior of batch outputs and the function optimum as a multivariate Gaussian, allowing a closed-form solution for mutual information. The optimization of this batch acquisition function, involving both continuous inputs and discrete fidelities, is tackled with an alternating optimization algorithm that cyclically updates each input-fidelity pair while fixing others, avoiding combinatorial explosion.",
    "Experimental Setup": "BMBO-DARN's surrogate learning performance was evaluated on two synthetic benchmark functions: Levy (two-fidelity) and Branin (three-fidelity), measuring normalized root-mean-square-error (nRMSE) and mean-negative-log-likelihood (MNLL). For real-world applications, it was applied to hyperparameter optimization for four machine learning models: Convolutional Neural Networks (CNN) for CIFAR-10 image classification (3 fidelities based on epochs, measured by negative log-loss), Online Latent Dirichlet Allocation (LDA) for 20NewsGroups text mining (3 fidelities based on epochs, measured by perplexity), XGBoost for diabetes diagnosis (3 fidelities based on weak learners, measured by log nRMSE), and Physics-Informed Neural Networks (PINN) for solving Burger's equation (3 fidelities based on L-BFGS iterations, measured by log nRMSE). The method was compared against state-of-the-art multi-fidelity BO algorithms (MF-GP-UCB, MF-MES, SHTL, DNN-MFBO), batch BO methods (MF-MES-Batch, SF-Batch), and popular hyperparameter tuning methods (SMAC3, Hyperband, BOHB), as well as a single-query version of BMBO-DARN (BMBO-DARN-1). Experiments used PyTorch with Hamiltorch for HMC sampling, typically with 2 hidden layers, 40 neurons, and specific HMC settings. All batch methods used a batch size of 5.",
    "Limitations": "The paper primarily highlights the limitations of existing multi-fidelity Bayesian Optimization methods, such as their tendency to oversimplify or ignore complex relationships between fidelities, and the inefficiency of one-by-one querying strategies. While the paper touts BMBO-DARN's efficiency, it implicitly acknowledges a potential computational overhead associated with Hamiltonian Monte Carlo (HMC) sampling for posterior inference by mentioning the possibility of switching to approximate inference methods like Stochastic Gradient HMC (SGHMC) 'as needed'. No other explicit limitations for BMBO-DARN itself are provided.",
    "Future Research Directions": "Not mentioned",
    "Experiment Code": null,
    "Experiment Result": null

    "Title": "Supervising the Multi-Fidelity Race of Hyperparameter Configurations",
    "Main Contributions": "The paper introduces DyHPO, a novel Bayesian Optimization (BO) method based on Gaussian Processes (GP) for multi-fidelity hyperparameter optimization (HPO). It addresses the sub-optimal budget allocation issue in existing gray-box HPO techniques. DyHPO proposes a deep kernel for Gaussian Processes that embeds learning curve dynamics and a new multi-budget acquisition function to dynamically allocate training budgets. The method demonstrates significant and statistically superior empirical gains over state-of-the-art HPO baselines across 50 diverse datasets (Tabular, Image, NLP) and deep learning architectures (MLP, CNN/NAS, RNN), while efficiently exploring promising configurations.",
    "Methodology": "DyHPO employs a Gaussian Process (GP) surrogate model with a novel deep kernel. This deep kernel, `K(θ, w) := k(φ(xi, Yi,j-1, j; w), φ(xi', Yi',j'-1, j'; w); θ)`, models the similarity between hyperparameter configurations by incorporating the configuration `xi`, the budget `j`, and the learning curve `Yi,j-1`. The feature extractor `φ` consists of linear and convolutional layers: the normalized budget `j` is concatenated with `xi` and fed into a linear layer, while the learning curve `Yi,j-1` is processed by a one-dimensional convolution followed by global max pooling; both representations are then combined by another linear layer. The parameters of the kernel (`θ` and `w`) are optimized via maximum likelihood estimation using gradient descent and Adam. For selecting the next configuration, DyHPO utilizes a multi-fidelity Expected Improvement (EIMF) acquisition function, `EIMF(x, j|D) = E[max{f(x, j) - ymax_j, 0}]`, which considers the best observed performance for a given budget `j` or across all budgets. The algorithm dynamically decides which candidate configuration to train for a small additional budget, slowly increasing the budget for promising configurations in a 'dynamic race' approach.",
    "Experimental Setup": "Experiments were conducted on an Amazon EC2 M5 Instance (m5.xlarge), with results reported as the mean of ten repetitions using regret and average rank as key metrics. Three diverse benchmarks were used: 1. LCBench: 35 tabular datasets with 2,000 feedforward neural network configurations per dataset, trained for 50 epochs, optimizing 7 numerical hyperparameters. 2. TaskSet: 12 NLP tasks with RNN architectures, using the Adam8p search space with 8 continuous hyperparameters, reporting scores every 200 iterations. 3. NAS-Bench-201: 15,625 precomputed neural architectures for CIFAR-10, CIFAR-100, and ImageNet, trained for 200 epochs, optimizing 6 categorical hyperparameters. DyHPO's performance was compared against seven state-of-the-art HPO baselines: Random Search, Hyperband, BOHB, DEHB, ASHA, MF-DNN, and Dragonfly (BOCA implementation). Hyperparameters for the deep kernel GP included an RBF kernel, dense layers of 128 and 256 units, and a convolutional layer with a kernel size of three and four filters. Training involved Adam with a learning rate of 0.1 and a batch size of 64, with early stopping if the likelihood did not improve for 10 epochs or after 1,000 epochs.",
    "Limitations": "The primary limitations include the current lack of tabular benchmarks specifically designed for Hyperparameter Optimization of very large deep learning models, such as Transformer-based architectures, which restricts the scope of current evaluation. Additionally, the pause and resume mechanism integral to DyHPO's training procedure is only applicable to parametric models, meaning that training for non-parametric models would require restarts. Lastly, for smaller datasets or tasks that train very quickly, the inherent computational overhead of model-based techniques like DyHPO might render simpler approaches, such as random search, more efficient and appealing.",
    "Future Research Directions": "Future research could involve adapting DyHPO for hyperparameter optimization of very large deep learning models, such as Transformers-based architectures, as currently suitable benchmarks are lacking. Exploring methods to extend DyHPO's pause and resume training capabilities to non-parametric models or models that do not inherently support incremental training is another direction. Additionally, optimizing DyHPO's computational overhead for fast-training tasks or small datasets could make it more competitive against simpler methods like random search in those scenarios. The paper also includes an invitation to the community to create sparse benchmarks with surrogates, rather than dense tabular ones, to promote energy efficiency.",
    "Experiment Code": "class FeatureExtractor(nn.Module):    \"\"\"    The feature extractor that is part of the deep kernel.    \"\"\"    def __init__(self, configuration):        super(FeatureExtractor, self).__init__()        self.configuration = configuration        self.nr_layers = configuration['nr_layers']        self.act_func = nn.LeakyReLU()        # adding one to the dimensionality of the initial input features        # for the concatenation with the budget.        initial_features = configuration['nr_initial_features'] + 1        self.fc1 = nn.Linear(initial_features, configuration['layer1_units'])        self.bn1 = nn.BatchNorm1d(configuration['layer1_units'])        for i in range(2, self.nr_layers):            setattr(                self,                f'fc{i + 1}',                nn.Linear(configuration[f'layer{i - 1}_units'], configuration[f'layer{i}_units']),            )            setattr(                self,                f'bn{i + 1}',                nn.BatchNorm1d(configuration[f'layer{i}_units']),            )        setattr(            self,            f'fc{self.nr_layers}',            nn.Linear(                configuration[f'layer{self.nr_layers - 1}_units'] +                configuration['cnn_nr_channels'],  # accounting for the learning curve features                configuration[f'layer{self.nr_layers}_units']            ),        )        self.cnn = nn.Sequential(            nn.Conv1d(in_channels=1, kernel_size=(configuration['cnn_kernel_size'],), out_channels=4),            nn.AdaptiveMaxPool1d(1),        )    def forward(self, x, budgets, learning_curves):        # add an extra dimensionality for the budget        # making it nr_rows x 1.        budgets = torch.unsqueeze(budgets, dim=1)        # concatenate budgets with examples        x = cat((x, budgets), dim=1)        x = self.fc1(x)        x = self.act_func(self.bn1(x))        for i in range(2, self.nr_layers):            x = self.act_func(                getattr(self, f'bn{i}')(                    getattr(self, f'fc{i}')(                        x                    )                )            )        # add an extra dimensionality for the learning curve        # making it nr_rows x 1 x lc_values.        learning_curves = torch.unsqueeze(learning_curves, 1)        lc_features = self.cnn(learning_curves)        # revert the output from the cnn into nr_rows x nr_kernels.        lc_features = torch.squeeze(lc_features, 2)        # put learning curve features into the last layer along with the higher level features.        x = cat((x, lc_features), dim=1)        x = self.act_func(getattr(self, f'fc{self.nr_layers}')(x))        return xclass GPRegressionModel(gpytorch.models.ExactGP):    \"\"\"    A simple GP model.    \"\"\"    def __init__(        self,        train_x: torch.Tensor,        train_y: torch.Tensor,        likelihood: gpytorch.likelihoods.GaussianLikelihood,    ):        \"\"\"        Constructor of the GPRegressionModel.        Args:            train_x: The initial train examples for the GP.            train_y: The initial train labels for the GP.            likelihood: The likelihood to be used.        \"\"\"        super(GPRegressionModel, self).__init__(train_x, train_y, likelihood)        self.mean_module = gpytorch.means.ConstantMean()        self.covar_module = gpytorch.kernels.ScaleKernel(gpytorch.kernels.RBFKernel())    def forward(self, x):        mean_x = self.mean_module(x)        covar_x = self.covar_module(x)        return gpytorch.distributions.MultivariateNormal(mean_x, covar_x)class DyHPO:    \"\"\"    The DyHPO DeepGP model.    \"\"\"    def __init__(        self,        configuration: Dict,        device: torch.device,        dataset_name: str = 'unknown',        output_path: str = '.',        seed: int = 11,    ):        \"\"\"        The constructor for the DyHPO model.        Args:            configuration: The configuration to be used                for the different parts of the surrogate.            device: The device where the experiments will be run on.            dataset_name: The name of the dataset for the current run.            output_path: The path where the intermediate/final results                will be stored.            seed: The seed that will be used to store the checkpoint                properly.        \"\"\"        super(DyHPO, self).__init__()        self.feature_extractor = FeatureExtractor(configuration)        self.batch_size = configuration['batch_size']        self.nr_epochs = configuration['nr_epochs']        self.early_stopping_patience = configuration['nr_patience_epochs']        self.refine_epochs = 50        self.dev = device        self.seed = seed        self.model, self.likelihood, self.mll = \n            self.get_model_likelihood_mll(                configuration[f'layer{self.feature_extractor.nr_layers}_units']            )        self.model.to(self.dev)        self.likelihood.to(self.dev)        self.feature_extractor.to(self.dev)        self.optimizer = torch.optim.Adam([            {'params': self.model.parameters(), 'lr': configuration['learning_rate']},            {'params': self.feature_extractor.parameters(), 'lr': configuration['learning_rate']}],        )        self.configuration = configuration        # the number of initial points for which we will retrain fully from scratch        # This is basically equal to the dimensionality of the search space + 1.        self.initial_nr_points = 10        # keeping track of the total hpo iterations. It will be used during the optimization        # process to switch from fully training the model, to refining.        self.iterations = 0        # flag for when the optimization of the model should start from scratch.        self.restart = True        self.logger = logging.getLogger(__name__)        self.checkpoint_path = os.path.join(            output_path,            'checkpoints',            f'{dataset_name}',            f'{self.seed}',        )        os.makedirs(self.checkpoint_path, exist_ok=True)        self.checkpoint_file = os.path.join(            self.checkpoint_path,            'checkpoint.pth'        )    def restart_optimization(self):        \"\"\"        Restart the surrogate model from scratch.        \"\"\"        self.feature_extractor = FeatureExtractor(self.configuration).to(self.dev)        self.model, self.likelihood, self.mll = \n            self.get_model_likelihood_mll(                self.configuration[f'layer{self.feature_extractor.nr_layers}_units'],            )        self.optimizer = torch.optim.Adam([            {'params': self.model.parameters(), 'lr': self.configuration['learning_rate']},            {'params': self.feature_extractor.parameters(), 'lr': self.configuration['learning_rate']}],        )    def get_model_likelihood_mll(        self,        train_size: int,    ) -> Tuple[GPRegressionModel, gpytorch.likelihoods.GaussianLikelihood, gpytorch.mlls.ExactMarginalLogLikelihood]:        \"\"\"        Called when the surrogate is first initialized or restarted.        Args:            train_size: The size of the current training set.        Returns:            model, likelihood, mll - The GP model, the likelihood and                the marginal likelihood.        \"\"\"        train_x = torch.ones(train_size, train_size).to(self.dev)        train_y = torch.ones(train_size).to(self.dev)        likelihood = gpytorch.likelihoods.GaussianLikelihood().to(self.dev)        model = GPRegressionModel(train_x=train_x, train_y=train_y, likelihood=likelihood).to(self.dev)        mll = gpytorch.mlls.ExactMarginalLogLikelihood(likelihood, model).to(self.dev)        return model, likelihood, mll    def train_pipeline(self, data: Dict[str, torch.Tensor], load_checkpoint: bool = False):        \"\"\"        Train the surrogate model.        Args:            data: A dictionary which has the training examples, training features,                training budgets and in the end the training curves.            load_checkpoint: A flag whether to load the state from a previous checkpoint,                or whether to start from scratch.        \"\"\"        self.iterations += 1        self.logger.debug(f'Starting iteration: {self.iterations}')        # whether the state has been changed. Basically, if a better loss was found during        # this optimization iteration then the state (weights) were changed.        weights_changed = False        if load_checkpoint:            try:                self.load_checkpoint()            except FileNotFoundError:                self.logger.error(f'No checkpoint file found at: {self.checkpoint_file}'                                  f'Training the GP from the beginning')        self.model.train()        self.likelihood.train()        self.feature_extractor.train()        self.optimizer = torch.optim.Adam([            {'params': self.model.parameters(), 'lr': self.configuration['learning_rate']},            {'params': self.feature_extractor.parameters(), 'lr': self.configuration['learning_rate']}],        )        X_train = data['X_train']        train_budgets = data['train_budgets']        train_curves = data['train_curves']        y_train = data['y_train']        initial_state = self.get_state()        training_errored = False        if self.restart:            self.restart_optimization()            nr_epochs = self.nr_epochs            # 2 cases where the statement below is hit.            # - We are switching from the full training phase in the beginning to refining.            # - We are restarting because our refining diverged            if self.initial_nr_points <= self.iterations:                self.restart = False        else:            nr_epochs = self.refine_epochs        # where the mean squared error will be stored        # when predicting on the train set        mse = 0.0        for epoch_nr in range(0, nr_epochs):            nr_examples_batch = X_train.size(dim=0)            # if only one example in the batch, skip the batch.            # Otherwise, the code will fail because of batchnorm            if nr_examples_batch == 1:                continue            # Zero backprop gradients            self.optimizer.zero_grad()            projected_x = self.feature_extractor(X_train, train_budgets, train_curves)            self.model.set_train_data(projected_x, y_train, strict=False)            output = self.model(projected_x)            try:                # Calc loss and backprop derivatives                loss = -self.mll(output, self.model.train_targets)                loss_value = loss.detach().to('cpu').item()                mse = gpytorch.metrics.mean_squared_error(output, self.model.train_targets)                self.logger.debug(                    f'Epoch {epoch_nr} - MSE {mse:.5f}, '                    f'Loss: {loss_value:.3f}, '                    f'lengthscale: {self.model.covar_module.base_kernel.lengthscale.item():.3f}, '                    f'noise: {self.model.likelihood.noise.item():.3f}, '                )                loss.backward()                self.optimizer.step()            except Exception as training_error:                self.logger.error(f'The following error happened while training: {training_error}')                # An error has happened, trigger the restart of the optimization and restart                # the model with default hyperparameters.                self.restart = True                training_errored = True                break        \"\"\"        # metric too high, time to restart, or we risk divergence        if mse > 0.15:            if not self.restart:                self.restart = True        \"\"\"        if training_errored:            self.save_checkpoint(initial_state)            self.load_checkpoint()    def predict_pipeline(        self,        train_data: Dict[str, torch.Tensor],        test_data: Dict[str, torch.Tensor],    ) -> Tuple[np.ndarray, np.ndarray]:        \"\"\"        Args:            train_data: A dictionary that has the training                examples, features, budgets and learning curves.            test_data: Same as for the training data, but it is                for the testing part and it does not feature labels.        Returns:            means, stds: The means of the predictions for the                testing points and the standard deviations.        \"\"\"        self.model.eval()        self.feature_extractor.eval()        self.likelihood.eval()        with torch.no_grad(): # gpytorch.settings.fast_pred_var():            projected_train_x = self.feature_extractor(                train_data['X_train'],                train_data['train_budgets'],                train_data['train_curves'],            )            self.model.set_train_data(inputs=projected_train_x, targets=train_data['y_train'], strict=False)            projected_test_x = self.feature_extractor(                test_data['X_test'],                test_data['test_budgets'],                test_data['test_curves'],            )            preds = self.likelihood(self.model(projected_test_x))        means = preds.mean.detach().to('cpu').numpy().reshape(-1, )        stds = preds.stddev.detach().to('cpu').numpy().reshape(-1, )        return means, stds    def load_checkpoint(self):        \"\"\"        Load the state from a previous checkpoint.        \"\"\"        checkpoint = torch.load(self.checkpoint_file)        self.model.load_state_dict(checkpoint['gp_state_dict'])        self.feature_extractor.load_state_dict(checkpoint['feature_extractor_state_dict'])        self.likelihood.load_state_dict(checkpoint['likelihood_state_dict'])    def save_checkpoint(self, state: Dict =None):        \"\"\"        Save the given state or the current state in a        checkpoint file.        Args:            state: The state to save, if none, it will            save the current state.        \"\"\"        if state is None:            torch.save(                self.get_state(),                self.checkpoint_file,            )        else:            torch.save(                state,                self.checkpoint_file,            )    def get_state(self) -> Dict[str, Dict]:        \"\"\"        Get the current state of the surrogate.        Returns:            current_state: A dictionary that represents                the current state of the surrogate model.        \"\"\"        current_state = {            'gp_state_dict': deepcopy(self.model.state_dict()),            'feature_extractor_state_dict': deepcopy(self.feature_extractor.state_dict()),            'likelihood_state_dict': deepcopy(self.likelihood.state_dict()),        }        return current_stateclass DyHPOAlgorithm:    def __init__(        self,        hp_candidates: np.ndarray,        log_indicator: List,        seed: int = 11,        max_benchmark_epochs: int = 52,        fantasize_step: int = 1,        minimization: bool = True,        total_budget: int = 500,        device: str = None,        dataset_name: str = 'unknown',        output_path: str = '.',        surrogate_config: dict = None,        verbose: bool = True,    ):        \"\"\"        Args:            hp_candidates: np.ndarray                The full list of hyperparameter candidates for                a given dataset.            log_indicator: List                A list with boolean values indicating if a                hyperparameter has been log sampled or not.            seed: int                The seed that will be used for the surrogate.            max_benchmark_epochs: int                The maximal budget that a hyperparameter configuration                has been evaluated in the benchmark for.            fantasize_step: int                The number of steps for which we are looking ahead to                evaluate the performance of a hpc.            minimization: bool                If the objective should be maximized or minimized.            total_budget: int                The total budget given for hyperparameter optimization.            device: str                The device where the experiment will be run on.            dataset_name: str                The name of the dataset that the experiment will be run on.            output_path: str                The path where all the output will be stored.            surrogate_config: dict                The model configurations for the surrogate.            verbose: boolean                If detailed information is preferred in the log file.        \"\"\"        torch.backends.cudnn.deterministic = True        torch.backends.cudnn.benchmark = False        torch.manual_seed(seed)        np.random.seed(seed)        if device is None:            self.dev = torch.device(                'cuda') if torch.cuda.is_available() else torch.device('cpu')        else:            self.dev = torch.device(device)        self.hp_candidates = hp_candidates        self.log_indicator = log_indicator        self.scaler = MinMaxScaler()        self.hp_candidates = self.preprocess_hp_candidates()        self.minimization = minimization        self.seed = seed        if verbose:            logging_level = logging.DEBUG        else:            logging_level = logging.INFO        self.logger = logging.getLogger()        logging.basicConfig(            format='%(levelname)s:%(asctime)s:%(message)s',            filename=f'dyhpo_surrogate_{dataset_name}_{seed}.log',            level=logging_level,        )        # the keys will be hyperparameter indices while the value        # will be a list with all the budgets evaluated for examples        # and with all performances for the performances        self.examples = dict()        self.performances = dict()        # set a seed already, so that it is deterministic when        # generating the seeds of the ensemble        torch.manual_seed(seed)        np.random.seed(seed)        self.max_benchmark_epochs = max_benchmark_epochs        self.total_budget = total_budget        self.fantasize_step = fantasize_step        self.nr_features = self.hp_candidates.shape[1]        initial_configurations_nr = 1        conf_individual_budget = 1        self.init_conf_indices = np.random.choice(self.hp_candidates.shape[0], initial_configurations_nr, replace=False)        self.init_budgets = [conf_individual_budget] * initial_configurations_nr        # with what percentage configurations will be taken randomly instead of being sampled from the model        self.fraction_random_configs = 0.1        self.model = None        # An index keeping track of where we are in the init_conf_indices        # list of hyperparmeters that are not sampled from the model.        self.initial_random_index = 0        if surrogate_config is None:            self.surrogate_config = {                'nr_layers': 2,                'nr_initial_features': self.nr_features,                'layer1_units': 64,                'layer2_units': 128,                'cnn_nr_channels': 4,                'cnn_kernel_size': 3,                'batch_size': 64,                'nr_epochs': 1000,                'nr_patience_epochs': 10,                'learning_rate': 0.001,            }        else:            self.surrogate_config = surrogate_config        # the incumbent value observed during the hpo process.        self.best_value_observed = np.NINF        # a set which will keep track of the hyperparameter configurations that diverge.        self.diverged_configs = set()        # info dict to drop every surrogate iteration        self.info_dict = dict()        # the start time for the overhead of every surrogate optimization iteration        # will be recorded here        self.suggest_time_duration = 0        # the total budget consumed so far        self.budget_spent = 0        self.output_path = output_path        self.dataset_name = dataset_name        self.no_improvement_threshold = int(self.max_benchmark_epochs + 0.2 * self.max_benchmark_epochs)        self.no_improvement_patience = 0    def _prepare_dataset_and_budgets(self) -> Dict[str, torch.Tensor]:        \"\"\"        Prepare the data that will be the input to the surrogate.        Returns:            data: A Dictionary that contains inside the training examples,            the budgets, the curves and lastly the labels.        \"\"\"        train_examples, train_labels, train_budgets, train_curves = self.history_configurations()        train_examples = np.array(train_examples, dtype=np.single)        train_labels = np.array(train_labels, dtype=np.single)        train_budgets = np.array(train_budgets, dtype=np.single)        train_curves = self.patch_curves_to_same_length(train_curves)        train_curves = np.array(train_curves, dtype=np.single)        # scale budgets to [0, 1]        train_budgets = train_budgets / self.max_benchmark_epochs        train_examples = torch.tensor(train_examples)        train_labels = torch.tensor(train_labels)        train_budgets = torch.tensor(train_budgets)        train_curves = torch.tensor(train_curves)        train_examples = train_examples.to(device=self.dev)        train_labels = train_labels.to(device=self.dev)        train_budgets = train_budgets.to(device=self.dev)        train_curves = train_curves.to(device=self.dev)        data = {            'X_train': train_examples,            'train_budgets': train_budgets,            'train_curves': train_curves,            'y_train': train_labels,        }        return data    def _train_surrogate(self):        \"\"\"        Train the surrogate model.        \"\"\"        data = self._prepare_dataset_and_budgets()        self.logger.info(f'Started training the model')        self.model.train_pipeline(            data,            load_checkpoint=False,        )    def _predict(self) -> Tuple[np.ndarray, np.ndarray, List, List]:        \"\"\"        Predict the performances of the hyperparameter configurations        as well as the standard deviations based on the surrogate model.        Returns:            mean_predictions, std_predictions, hp_indices, non_scaled_budgets:                The mean predictions and the standard deviations over                all model predictions for the given hyperparameter                configurations with their associated indices, scaled and                non-scaled budgets.        \"\"\"        configurations, hp_indices, budgets, learning_curves = self.generate_candidate_configurations()        budgets = np.array(budgets, dtype=np.single)        non_scaled_budgets = copy.deepcopy(budgets)        # scale budgets to [0, 1]        budgets = budgets / self.max_benchmark_epochs        configurations = np.array(configurations, dtype=np.single)        configurations = torch.tensor(configurations)        configurations = configurations.to(device=self.dev)        budgets = torch.tensor(budgets)        budgets = budgets.to(device=self.dev)        learning_curves = self.patch_curves_to_same_length(learning_curves)        learning_curves = np.array(learning_curves, dtype=np.single)        learning_curves = torch.tensor(learning_curves)        learning_curves = learning_curves.to(device=self.dev)        train_data = self._prepare_dataset_and_budgets()        test_data = {            'X_test': configurations,            'test_budgets': budgets,            'test_curves': learning_curves,        }        mean_predictions, std_predictions = self.model.predict_pipeline(train_data, test_data)        return mean_predictions, std_predictions, hp_indices, non_scaled_budgets    def suggest(self) -> Tuple[int, int]:        \"\"\"        Suggest a hyperparameter configuration to be evaluated next.        Returns:            best_config_index, budget: The index of the hyperparamter                configuration to be evaluated and the budget for                what it is going to be evaluated for.        \"\"\"        suggest_time_start = time.time()        # check if we still have random hyperparameters to evaluate        if self.initial_random_index < len(self.init_conf_indices):            self.logger.info(                'Not enough configurations to build a model. '                'Returning randomly sampled configuration'            )            random_indice = self.init_conf_indices[self.initial_random_index]            budget = self.init_budgets[self.initial_random_index]            self.initial_random_index += 1            return random_indice, budget        else:            mean_predictions, std_predictions, hp_indices, non_scaled_budgets = self._predict()            best_prediction_index = self.find_suggested_config(                mean_predictions,                std_predictions,                non_scaled_budgets,            )            \"\"\"            the best prediction index is not always matching with the actual hp index.            Since when evaluating the acq function, we do not consider hyperparameter            candidates that diverged or that are evaluated fully.            \"\"\"            best_config_index = hp_indices[best_prediction_index]            # decide for what budget we will evaluate the most            # promising hyperparameter configuration next.            if best_config_index in self.examples:                evaluated_budgets = self.examples[best_config_index]                max_budget = max(evaluated_budgets)                budget = max_budget + self.fantasize_step                # this would only trigger if fantasize_step is bigger                # than 1                if budget > self.max_benchmark_epochs:                    budget = self.max_benchmark_epochs            else:                budget = self.fantasize_step        suggest_time_end = time.time()        self.suggest_time_duration = suggest_time_end - suggest_time_start        self.budget_spent += self.fantasize_step        # exhausted hpo budget, finish.        if self.budget_spent > self.total_budget:            exit(0)        return best_config_index, budget    def observe(        self,        hp_index: int,        b: int,        learning_curve: np.ndarray,        alg_time: Optional[float] = None,    ):        \"\"\"        Args:            hp_index: The index of the evaluated hyperparameter configuration.            b: The budget for which the hyperparameter configuration was evaluated.            learning_curve: The learning curve of the hyperparameter configuration.            alg_time: The time taken from the algorithm to evaluate the hp configuration.        \"\"\"        score = learning_curve[-1]        # if y is an undefined value, append 0 as the overhead since we finish here.        if np.isnan(learning_curve).any():            self.update_info_dict(hp_index, b, np.nan, 0)            self.diverged_configs.add(hp_index)            return        observe_time_start = time.time()        self.examples[hp_index] = np.arange(1, b + 1).tolist()        self.performances[hp_index] = learning_curve        if self.best_value_observed < score:            self.best_value_observed = score            self.no_improvement_patience = 0        else:            self.no_improvement_patience += 1        observe_time_end = time.time()        train_time_duration = 0        # initialization phase over. Now we can sample from the model.        if self.initial_random_index >= len(self.init_conf_indices):            train_time_start = time.time()            # create the model for the first time            if self.model is None:                # Starting a model from scratch                self.model = DyHPO(                    self.surrogate_config,                    self.dev,                    self.dataset_name,                    self.output_path,                    self.seed,                )            if self.no_improvement_patience == self.no_improvement_threshold:                self.model.restart = True            self._train_surrogate()            train_time_end = time.time()            train_time_duration = train_time_end - train_time_start        observe_time_duration = observe_time_end - observe_time_start        total_duration = observe_time_duration + self.suggest_time_duration + train_time_duration        if alg_time is not None:            total_duration = total_duration + alg_time        self.update_info_dict(hp_index, b, score, total_duration)    def prepare_examples(self, hp_indices: List) -> List[np.ndarray]:        \"\"\"        Prepare the examples to be given to the surrogate model.        Args:            hp_indices: The list of hp indices that are already evaluated.        Returns:            examples: A list of the hyperparameter configurations.        \"\"\"        examples = []        for hp_index in hp_indices:            examples.append(self.hp_candidates[hp_index])        return examples    def generate_candidate_configurations(        self,    ) -> Tuple[List, List, List, List]:        \"\"\"        Generate candidate configurations that will be        fantasized upon.        Returns:            (configurations, hp_indices, hp_budgets, learning_curves): Tuple                A tuple of configurations, their indices in the hp list                and the budgets that they should be fantasized upon.        \"\"\"        hp_indices = []        hp_budgets = []        learning_curves = []        for hp_index in range(0, self.hp_candidates.shape[0]):            if hp_index in self.examples:                budgets = self.examples[hp_index]                # Take the max budget evaluated for a certain hpc                max_budget = max(budgets)                next_budget = max_budget + self.fantasize_step                # take the learning curve until the point we have evaluated so far                curve = self.performances[hp_index][:max_budget]                # if the curve is shorter than the length of the kernel size,                # pad it with zeros                difference_curve_length = self.surrogate_config['cnn_kernel_size'] - len(curve)                if difference_curve_length > 0:                    curve.extend([0.0] * difference_curve_length)            else:                # The hpc was not evaluated before, so fantasize its                # performance                next_budget = self.fantasize_step                curve = [0, 0, 0]            # this hyperparameter configuration is not evaluated fully            if next_budget <= self.max_benchmark_epochs:                hp_indices.append(hp_index)                hp_budgets.append(next_budget)                learning_curves.append(curve)        configurations = self.prepare_examples(hp_indices)        return configurations, hp_indices, hp_budgets, learning_curves    def history_configurations(        self,    ) -> Tuple[List, List, List, List]:        \"\"\"        Generate the configurations, labels, budgets and curves based on        the history of evaluated configurations.        Returns:            (train_examples, train_labels, train_budgets, train_curves):                A tuple of examples, labels, budgets and curves for the                configurations evaluated so far.        \"\"\"        train_examples = []        train_labels = []        train_budgets = []        train_curves = []        for hp_index in self.examples:            budgets = self.examples[hp_index]            performances = self.performances[hp_index]            example = self.hp_candidates[hp_index]            for budget, performance in zip(budgets, performances):                train_examples.append(example)                train_budgets.append(budget)                train_labels.append(performance)                train_curve = performances[:budget - 1] if budget > 1 else [0.0]                difference_curve_length = self.surrogate_config['cnn_kernel_size'] - len(train_curve)                if difference_curve_length > 0:                    train_curve.extend([0.0] * difference_curve_length)                train_curves.append(train_curve)        return train_examples, train_labels, train_budgets, train_curves    def acq(        self,        best_value: float,        mean: float,        std: float,        explore_factor: Optional[float] = 0.25,        acq_fc: str = 'ei',    ) -> float:        \"\"\"        The acquisition function that will be called        to evaluate the score of a hyperparameter configuration.        Parameters        ----------        best_value: float            Best observed function evaluation. Individual per fidelity.        mean: float            Point mean of the posterior process.        std: float            Point std of the posterior process.        explore_factor: float            The exploration factor for when ucb is used as the            acquisition function.        ei_calibration_factor: float            The factor used to calibrate expected improvement.        acq_fc: str            The type of acquisition function to use.        Returns        -------        acq_value: float            The value of the acquisition function.        \"\"\"        if acq_fc == 'ei':            if std == 0:                return 0            z = (mean - best_value) / std            acq_value = (mean - best_value) * norm.cdf(z) + std * norm.pdf(z)        elif acq_fc == 'ucb':            acq_value = mean + explore_factor * std        elif acq_fc == 'thompson':            acq_value = np.random.normal(mean, std)        elif acq_fc == 'exploit':            acq_value = mean        else:            raise NotImplementedError(                f'Acquisition function {acq_fc} has not been'                f'implemented',            )        return acq_value    def find_suggested_config(        self,        mean_predictions: np.ndarray,        mean_stds: np.ndarray,        budgets: List,    ) -> int:        \"\"\"        Find the hyperparameter configuration that has the highest score        with the acquisition function.        Args:            mean_predictions: The mean predictions of the posterior.            mean_stds: The mean standard deviations of the posterior.            budgets: The next budgets that the hyperparameter configurations                will be evaluated for.        Returns:            best_index: The index of the hyperparameter configuration with the                highest score.        \"\"\"        highest_acq_value = np.NINF        best_index = -1        index = 0        for mean_value, std in zip(mean_predictions, mean_stds):            budget = int(budgets[index])            best_value = self.calculate_fidelity_ymax(budget)            acq_value = self.acq(best_value, mean_value, std, acq_fc='ei')            if acq_value > highest_acq_value:                highest_acq_value = acq_value                best_index = index            index += 1        return best_index    def calculate_fidelity_ymax(self, fidelity: int):        \"\"\"        Find ymax for a given fidelity level.        If there are hyperparameters evaluated for that fidelity        take the maximum from their values. Otherwise, take        the maximum from all previous fidelity levels for the        hyperparameters that we have evaluated.        Args:            fidelity: The fidelity of the hyperparameter                configuration.        Returns:            best_value: The best value seen so far for the                given fidelity.        \"\"\"        exact_fidelity_config_values = []        lower_fidelity_config_values = []        for example_index in self.examples.keys():            try:                performance = self.performances[example_index][fidelity - 1]                exact_fidelity_config_values.append(performance)            except IndexError:                learning_curve = self.performances[example_index]                # The hyperparameter was not evaluated until fidelity, or more.                # Take the maximum value from the curve.                lower_fidelity_config_values.append(max(learning_curve))        if len(exact_fidelity_config_values) > 0:            # lowest error corresponds to best value            best_value = max(exact_fidelity_config_values)        else:            best_value = max(lower_fidelity_config_values)        return best_value    def preprocess_hp_candidates(self) -> List:        \"\"\"        Preprocess the list of all hyperparameter candidates        by  performing a log transform for the hyperparameters that        were log sampled.        Returns:            log_hp_candidates: The list of all hyperparameter configurations                where hyperparameters that were log sampled are log transformed.        \"\"\"        log_hp_candidates = []        for hp_candidate in self.hp_candidates:            new_hp_candidate = []            for index, hp_value in enumerate(hp_candidate):                new_hp_candidate.append(math.log(hp_value) if self.log_indicator[index] else hp_value)        log_hp_candidates = np.array(log_hp_candidates)        # scaler for the hp configurations        log_hp_candidates = self.scaler.fit_transform(log_hp_candidates)        return log_hp_candidates    @staticmethod    def patch_curves_to_same_length(curves):        \"\"\"        Patch the given curves to the same length.        Finds the maximum curve length and patches all        other curves that are shorter in length with zeroes.        Args:            curves: The given hyperparameter curves.        Returns:            curves: The updated array where the learning                curves are of the same length.        \"\"\"        max_curve_length = 0        for curve in curves:            if len(curve) > max_curve_length:                max_curve_length = len(curve)        for curve in curves:            difference = max_curve_length - len(curve)            if difference > 0:                curve.extend([0.0] * difference)        return curves",
