entropy(self):\n        entropy = 0.0\n        for probs in product(*(theta[theta>0.0] for theta in self._theta)):\n            prob = np.prod(probs)\n            entropy -= prob * np.log(prob)\n        return entropy\n    def mle(self):\n        return np.prod([theta.max() for theta in self._theta])\n    def __init__(self, server, configs, eta0='auto', sched='auto', cutoff=0.0, baseline=0.0, diff=False,):\n        self._server = server\n        self._configs = configs\n        self._grid = [] if type(configs) == list else sorted(configs.keys())\n        sizes = [len(configs[param]) for param in self._grid] if self._grid else [len(configs)]\n        self._eta0 = [np.sqrt(2.0 * np.log(size)) if eta0 == 'auto' else eta0 for size in sizes]\n        self._sched = sched\n        self._cutoff = cutoff\n        self._baseline = baseline\n        self._diff = diff\n        self._z = [np.full(size, -np.log(size)) for size in sizes]\n        self._theta = [np.exp(z) for z in self._configs[param][theta.argmax()] \n                        for theta, param in zip(self._theta, self._grid)}\n            return self._configs[self._theta[0].argmax()]\n step(self):\n        index = []\n        before, after, weight = self._server.communication_round(lambda: self.sample(_index=index))        \n        before, after = np.array(before), np.array(after)\n        weight = np.array(weight, dtype=np.float64) / sum(weight)\n        if self._trace['refine']:\n            trace = self.trace('refine')\n            if self._diff:\n                trace -= self.trace('global')\n            baseline = discounted_mean(trace, self._baseline)\n        else:\n            baseline = 0.0\n        self._trace['global'].append(np.inner(before, weight))\n        self._trace['refine'].append(np.inner(after, weight))\n        if not index:\n            self._trace['entropy'].append(0.0)\n            self._trace['mle'].append(1.0)\n            return\n        for i, (z, theta) in enumerate(zip(self._z, self._theta)):\n            grad = np.zeros(len(z))\n            for idx, s, w in zip(index, after-before if self._diff else after, weight):\n                grad[idx[i]] += w * (s - baseline) / theta[idx[i]]\n            if self._sched == 'adaptive':\n                self._store[i] += norm(grad, float('inf')) ** 2\n                denom = np.sqrt(self._store[i])\n            elif self._sched == 'aggressive':\n                denom = 1.0 if np.all(grad == 0.0) else norm(grad, float('inf'))\n            elif self._sched == 'auto':\n                self._store[i] += 1.0\n                denom = np.sqrt(self._store[i])\n            elif self._sched == 'constant':\n                denom = 1.0\n            elif self._sched == 'scale':\n                denom = 1.0 / np.sqrt(2.0 * np.log(len(grad))) if len(grad) > 1 else float('inf')\n            else:\n                raise NotImplementedError\n            eta = self._eta0[i] / denom\n            z -= eta * grad\n            z -= logsumexp(z)\n            self._theta[i] = np.exp(z)\n        self._trace['entropy'].append(self.entropy())\n        self._trace['mle'].append(self.mle())\n        if self._trace['entropy'][-1] < self._cutoff:\n            self.stop()\n    def test(self, mle=False):\n        state = self._set_test_state()\n     "Title": "FedHPO-Bench: A Benchmark Suite for Federated Hyperparameter Optimization",
    "Main Contributions": "The paper identifies the uniqueness of Hyperparameter Optimization (HPO) for Federated Learning (FL) algorithms (FedHPO), highlighting that existing HPO benchmarks are inadequate for this setting due to FL's distributed nature and client heterogeneity. To address this, the authors propose and implement FEDHPO-B, a comprehensive, efficient, and extensible benchmark suite for FedHPO. Key contributions include incorporating diverse FL tasks, enabling efficient function evaluations through tabular, surrogate, and raw modes (with a novel system model for realistic time estimation), and easing extensibility by building upon the FederatedScope platform. Experimental findings demonstrate that traditional multi-fidelity HPO methods generally outperform single-fidelity ones in FL, concurrent exploration significantly improves generalization performance for FedAvg, and the new fidelity dimension 'sample_rate' can be balanced with '#rounds' to achieve economical accuracy-efficiency trade-offs based on system conditions.",
    "Methodology": "FEDHPO-B is built upon the FederatedScope (FS) FL platform and offers three evaluation modes: 'tabular', 'surrogate', and 'raw'. The 'tabular' mode provides pre-computed results from grid searches over discrete hyperparameter spaces, stored in lookup tables. The 'surrogate' mode uses random forest models, trained on the tabular data, to enable efficient inference for arbitrary hyperparameter configurations. The 'raw' mode allows for true execution of FL algorithms in containerized environments for user-defined search spaces. A novel system model is implemented to estimate realistic execution times in the 'raw' mode, considering communication bottlenecks (server, client bandwidth) and client computational heterogeneity (modeled with exponential distribution for straggler estimation). For integrating FedHPO methods, a general algorithmic view unifying several approaches (e.g., FedEx, FTS) is proposed, facilitating extensibility. The benchmark evaluated traditional HPO methods (Random Search, Differential Evolution, Bayesian Optimization with GP, RF, KDE) and multi-fidelity optimizers (Hyperband, BOHB, DEHB, TPE with median stopping/Hyperband) on various FL tasks.",
    "Experimental Setup": "The experimental setup includes a comprehensive collection of FL tasks spanning diverse data domains (images, sentences, graphs, tabular) and model architectures (CNNs, BERT, GNNs, LR, MLPs). Specific datasets used are FEMNIST (LEAF), split Cora (FS-GNN), CIFAR-10, SST-2, CoLA (GLUE), and seven tabular datasets from OpenML, all configured with Non-IIDness among clients. The FL algorithms tested are FedAvg and FedOPT. Hyperparameters optimized include client-side (e.g., learning rate, local update steps, batch size, weight decay, dropout) and server-side (e.g., learning rate, momentum) parameters, along with fidelity dimensions like '#round' and 'sample_rate'. Experiments for traditional HPO methods involved 20 FL tasks, repeated five times, measuring best-seen validation loss and comparing mean ranks and using sign tests. Concurrent exploration studies were conducted on FedAvg and FedOPT with CNN on FEMNIST in a one-shot optimization setting, comparing RS, SHA, RS+FedEx, and SHA+FedEx, with results averaged over three repetitions. Studies on the new fidelity dimension explored Hyperband performance with varying 'sample_rate' under simulated 'bad network' and 'good network' conditions using the system model.",
    "Limitations": "Existing HPO benchmarks are unsuitable for FedHPO due to their focus on centralized learning and inability to incorporate FedHPO-specific methods. The tabular mode of FEDHPO-B discretizes the search space, limiting queries to predefined grids. The raw mode, while providing accurate results, is computationally expensive. Due to computing resource constraints, the current tabular and surrogate modes for the CNN benchmark only support FedAvg, and some raw mode experimental results are incomplete. The one-shot optimization setting for concurrent exploration studies severely limited initial configuration choices, which sometimes prevented satisfactory performance, especially for critical hyperparameters like server-side learning rate in FedOPT. The current version of FEDHPO-B does not address the risk of privacy leakage caused by HPO methods, lacking related metrics and testbeds.",
    "Future Research Directions": "Future work for FEDHPO-B includes continuously integrating more benchmarks and emerging learning paradigms, such as federated reinforcement learning, federated unsupervised representation learning, and federated hetero-task learning. The authors also plan to extend FEDHPO-B to include different FL settings like HPO for vertical FL. Addressing privacy concerns is a key future direction, which involves providing metrics and testbeds for privacy leakage risks associated with HPO methods. Further improvements include augmenting the lookup tables in tabular mode with a denser grid and incorporating advanced surrogate models like neural network-based approaches (e.g., YAHPO Gym). Additionally, the authors intend to supplement the currently incomplete experimental results for raw mode and the CNN benchmark with FedOPT.",

    "Title": "Hyperparameter Optimization through Neural Network Partitioning",
    "Main Contributions": "The paper proposes \"partitioned neural networks,\" a novel and efficient method for hyperparameter optimization (HPO) in neural networks. It addresses the high computational cost of traditional HPO methods (requiring validation sets and multiple training runs) and the intractability of marginal likelihood computation for deep learning. Key contributions include enabling HPO within a single training run, eliminating the need for a separate validation set, and offering significant computational advantages over existing marginal likelihood approximation methods. The method is broadly applicable to various hyperparameters (e.g., input masks, data augmentations, feature extractors, dropout rates) and is particularly beneficial for challenging scenarios like federated learning, where it also reduces communication overhead and improves model generalization.",
    "Methodology": "The core methodology is inspired by marginal likelihood and the \"learning speed\" perspective. It involves partitioning both the training data into C shards (D1, ..., DC) and the neural network parameters into C partitions (w1, ..., wC). For each k, a subnetwork w(k)s is constructed by using the first k parameter partitions (w1, ..., wk) and setting the remaining partitions (wk+1, ..., wC) to default values (e.g., initialization or zero). Parameter partitions wk are optimized on data shards D1:k using their respective subnetworks w(k)s. Hyperparameters ψ are optimized using an objective LML (D,ψ) = ∑k Eqk−1(w) [log p(Dk|w,ψ)], which effectively measures the 'out-of-training-sample' loss on data shard Dk using the (k-1)-th subnetwork w(k-1)s. This objective allows for differentiable optimization via stochastic gradient descent. The primary partitioning scheme used is 'random weight partitioning,' where a fixed proportion of weights in each layer is randomly assigned to a partition. For federated learning, clients are assigned to data chunks, and a client in chunk k updates partitions wk:C and hyperparameters ψ, with only the modified parameters communicated to the server.",
    "Experimental Setup": "The method was evaluated on various tasks: toy input selection (non-differentiable and differentiable mask learning), learning affine image augmentations (rotation, translation, scale, shear), optimizing general feature extractors (stages of a Wide ResNet-20), and learning dropout rates. Datasets used include a synthetic input selection dataset, MNIST, RotMNIST, CIFAR10, RotCIFAR10, TinyImagenet, and RotTinyImagenet. For federated learning, datasets were synthetically split to be non-i.i.d. with label-skew and rotation-skew. Architectures varied from fully connected neural networks (MLPs) to fixupResNets (ResNet-8, ResNet-14), ResNet-50 with GroupNorm, Wide ResNet-20, and a CNN for MNIST. Baselines included post-hoc diagonal Laplace, standard training, Augerino, Differentiable Laplace, Last-layer marginal-likelihood, traditional training/validation split with finetuning, FedAvg, and FedAvg + Augerino. Performance was measured by test accuracy, test log-likelihood, validation accuracy (for FL), and communication costs (for FL). Training involved Adam and SGD optimizers with specific learning rates, momentum, and weight decay schedules. Partitioning typically used 2 or 3 chunks, with data and parameter proportions matched (e.g., [80%,10%,10%]).",
    "Limitations": "The proposed method introduces several limitations. It requires an additional forward-backward pass to update hyperparameters, although this overhead is significantly less than existing marginal likelihood-based approaches. Empirically, partitioned networks often demand more training iterations to converge. Furthermore, partitioning inherently constrains the network's capacity, which can lead to a slight performance degradation compared to a full, non-partitioned network with perfectly optimized hyperparameters. The choice of the partitioning strategy (number of chunks, data, and parameter proportions) becomes an additional hyperparameter that may require tuning for optimal performance. Lastly, for deep normalization-free architectures like FixUp, setting default weight values to initialised values is necessary for training stability, potentially doubling the memory needed for storing parameters, though clever implementations can mitigate this.",
    "Future Research Directions": "Future research could explore several avenues. Investigating methods to mitigate the performance loss incurred due to constrained network capacity, such as adjusting training rounds or initially increasing overall network capacity, is a promising direction. Exploring dynamic network parameter partitioning during training, rather than static partitioning prior to training, could offer more adaptability. Developing alternative partitioning schemes that allow for updating multiple parameter partitions within a single batch could further reduce computational overhead. In the federated learning context, exploring different sequential updating schemes for clients could be beneficial. Additionally, researching methods to accumulate gradients from different chunks for hyperparameter updates to achieve lower variance, and investigating the impact of interleaving hyperparameter updates at less frequent intervals to reduce computational overhead are also potential areas for extension.",
    "Experiment Code": null,
    "Experiment Result": null

    "Title": "Single-shot General Hyper-parameter Optimization for Federated Learning",
    "Main Contributions": "The paper introduces Federated Loss Surface Aggregation (FLoRA), a general framework for single-shot hyper-parameter optimization (FL-HPO) in federated learning. FLoRA addresses the FL-HPO problem by enabling the selection of a single set of optimal hyper-parameters for federated training, even for non-neural network models and tabular data, with minimal communication overhead. A key contribution is the theoretical characterization of FLoRA's optimality gap, explicitly accounting for the heterogeneous, non-IID nature of local data distributions. Empirical evaluations demonstrate FLoRA's significant accuracy improvements over baseline configurations and its robustness to an increasing number of participating parties.",
    "Methodology": "FLoRA leverages meta-learning techniques by utilizing local and asynchronous HPO at each party. Each party independently performs HPO (e.g., Bayesian Optimization) to generate a set of (hyper-parameter, local loss) pairs. These pairs are then collected by a central aggregator, which constructs a unified loss surface across the entire hyper-parameter space. Four distinct methods are proposed for constructing this unified loss surface: Single Global Model (SGM), SGM with Uncertainty (SGM+U), Maximum of Per-Party Local Models (MPLM), and Average of Per-Party Local Models (APLM). The aggregator then selects the optimal hyper-parameter configuration from this surface, which is subsequently used for a single round of federated training.",
    "Experimental Setup": "The empirical evaluation of FLoRA involved Histogram-based Gradient Boosted Decision Trees (HGB), Support Vector Machines (SVMs) with RBF kernels, and Multi-layered Perceptrons (MLPs), using their scikit-learn implementations. Seven binary classification datasets from OpenML were used. The baseline for comparison was the default hyper-parameter configuration provided by scikit-learn. Performance was measured using balanced accuracy and relative regret. Experiments were initially conducted by emulating federated training with pooled data and 10-fold cross-validation for statistical robustness. Further tests explored the impact of increasing the number of parties (from 3 to 100), the number of local HPO rounds, and the communication overhead (by sending only the 'best' HP-loss pairs). FLoRA was also validated in a real IBM FL testbed using a global holdout set.",
    "Limitations": "The current framework focuses solely on optimizing global model hyper-parameters, explicitly deferring the optimization of aggregator-specific hyper-parameters for future work. It assumes the existence of a global holdout/validation set for evaluating the final federated model, but this set is not accessible during the HPO process itself. The theoretical analysis relies on Lipschitzness assumptions for loss functions and loss surfaces, which, while relaxable, can be a strong assumption for complex, mixed hyper-parameter spaces. Empirically, some loss surface aggregation methods (e.g., MPLM) showed degraded performance in challenging scenarios with high data heterogeneity among parties or limited potential for improvement.",
    "Future Research Directions": "The paper explicitly states that future work will include Hyper-parameter Optimization (HPO) for aggregator-specific hyper-parameters (φ). Additionally, the theoretical analysis could be extended by relaxing the Lipschitzness assumption to milder conditions such as modulus of continuity. Implicitly, based on empirical findings, future research could also focus on developing more robust loss surface aggregation methods that perform consistently well even in scenarios with high data heterogeneity or when the potential for hyper-parameter improvement is limited.",
    "Experiment Code": "import numpy as np\\nfrom scipy.interpolate import interp1d\\nfrom skopt import Optimizer as SkOpt\\nfrom skopt.space import Categorical, Integer, Real\\n\\nfrom bayesmark.abstract_optimizer import AbstractOptimizer\\nfrom bayesmark.experiment import experiment_main\\n\\n\\nclass ScikitOptimizer(AbstractOptimizer):\\n    primary_import = \"scikit-optimize\"\\n\\n    def __init__(self, api_config, base_estimator=\"GBRT\", acq_func=\"EI\", n_initial_points=5):\\n        \"\"\"Build wrapper class to use an optimizer in benchmark.\\n\\n        Parameters\\n        ----------\\n        api_config : dict-like of dict-like\\n            Configuration of the optimization variables. See API description.\\n        base_estimator : {'GP', 'RF', 'ET', 'GBRT'}\\n            How to estimate the objective function.\\n        acq_func : {'LCB', 'EI', 'PI', 'gp_hedge', 'EIps', 'PIps'}\\n            Acquisition objective to decide next suggestion.\\n        n_initial_points : int\\n            Number of points to sample randomly before actual Bayes opt.\\n        \"\"\"\\n        AbstractOptimizer.__init__(self, api_config)\\n\\n        dimensions, self.round_to_values = ScikitOptimizer.get_sk_dimensions(api_config)\\n\\n        # Older versions of skopt don't copy over the dimensions names during\\n        # normalization and hence the names are missing in\\n        # self.skopt.space.dimensions. Therefore, we save our own copy of\\n        # dimensions list to be safe. If we can commit to using the newer\\n        # versions of skopt we can delete self.dimensions.\\n        self.dimensions_list = tuple(dd.name for dd in dimensions)\\n\\n        self.skopt = SkOpt(\\n            dimensions,\\n            n_initial_points=n_initial_points,\\n            base_estimator=base_estimator,\\n            acq_func=acq_func,\\n            acq_optimizer=\"sampling\",\\n            acq_func_kwargs={},\\n            acq_optimizer_kwargs={},\\n        )\\n\\n    @staticmethod\\n    def get_sk_dimensions(api_config, transform=\"normalize\"):\\n        \"\"\"Help routine to setup skopt search space in constructor.\\n\\n        Take api_config as argument so this can be static.\\n        \"\"\"\\n        # The ordering of iteration prob makes no difference, but just to be\\n        # safe and consistnent with space.py, I will make sorted.\\n        param_list = sorted(api_config.keys())\\n\\n        sk_dims = []\\n        round_to_values = {}\\n        for param_name in param_list:\\n            param_config = api_config[param_name]\\n\\n            param_type = param_config[\"type\"]\\n\\n            param_space = param_config.get(\"space\", None)\\n            param_range = param_config.get(\"range\", None)\\n            param_values = param_config.get(\"values\", None)\\n\\n            # Some setup for case that whitelist of values is provided:\\n            values_only_type = param_type in (\"cat\", \"ordinal\")\\n            if (param_values is not None) and (not values_only_type):\\n                assert param_range is None\\n                param_values = np.unique(param_values)\\n                param_range = (param_values[0], param_values[-1])\\n                round_to_values[param_name] = interp1d(\\n                    param_values, param_values, kind=\"nearest\", fill_value=\"extrapolate\"\\n                )\\n\\n            if param_type == \"int\":\\n                # Integer space in sklearn does not support any warping => Need\\n                # to leave the warping as linear in skopt.\\n                sk_dims.append(Integer(param_range[0], param_range[-1], transform=transform, name=param_name))\\n            elif param_type == \"bool\":\\n                assert param_range is None\\n                assert param_values is None\\n                sk_dims.append(Integer(0, 1, transform=transform, name=param_name))\\n            elif param_type in (\"cat\", \"ordinal\"):\\n                assert param_range is None\\n                # Leave x-form to one-hot as per skopt default\\n                sk_dims.append(Categorical(param_values, name=param_name))\\n            elif param_type == \"real\":\\n                # Skopt doesn't support all our warpings, so need to pick\\n                # closest substitute it does support.\\n                prior = \"log-uniform\" if param_space in (\"log\", \"logit\") else \"uniform\"\\n                sk_dims.append(Real(param_range[0], param_range[-1], prior=prior, transform=transform, name=param_name))\\n            else:\\n                assert False, \"type %s not handled in API\" % param_type\\n        return sk_dims, round_to_values\\n\\n    def suggest(self, n_suggestions=1):\\n        \"\"\"Get a suggestion from the optimizer.\\n\\n        Parameters\\n        ----------\\n        n_suggestions : int\\n            Desired number of parallel suggestions in the output\\n\\n        Returns\\n        -------\\n        next_guess : list of dict\\n            List of `n_suggestions` suggestions to evaluate the objective\\n            function. Each suggestion is a dictionary where each key\\n            corresponds to a parameter being optimized.\\n        \"\"\"\\n        # First get list of lists from skopt.ask()\\n        next_guess = self.skopt.ask(n_points=n_suggestions)\\n        # Then convert to list of dicts\\n        next_guess = [dict(zip(self.dimensions_list, x)) for x in next_guess]\\n\\n        # Now do the rounding, custom rounding is not supported in skopt. Note\\n        # that there is not nec a round function for each dimension here.\\n        for param_name, round_f in self.round_to_values.items():\\n            for xx in next_guess:\\n                xx[param_name] = round_f(xx[param_name])\\n        return next_guess\\n\\n    def observe(self, X, y):\\n        \"\"\"Send an observation of a suggestion back to the optimizer.\\n\\n        Parameters\\n        ----------\\n        X : list of dict-like\\n            Places where the objective function has already been evaluated.\\n            Each suggestion is a dictionary where each key corresponds to a\\n            parameter being optimized.\\n        y : array-like, shape (n,)\\n            Corresponding values where objective has been evaluated\\n        \"\"\"\\n        # Supposedly skopt can handle blocks, but not sure about interface for\\n        # that. Just do loop to be safe for now.\\n        for xx, yy in zip(X, y):\\n            # skopt needs lists instead of dicts\\n            xx = [xx[dim_name] for dim_name in self.dimensions_list]\\n            # Just ignore, any inf observations we got, unclear if right thing\\n            if np.isfinite(yy):\\n                self.skopt.tell(xx, yy)\\n\\n\\nif __name__ == \"__main__\":\\n    experiment_main(ScikitOptimizer)",
    "Experiment Result": "This code (`skopt/optimizer.py`) implements a Bayesian Optimization (BO) optimizer using the `scikit-optimize` library. This can serve as the 'local HPO' component for individual parties in the FLoRA framework, which, according to the method description, each party would independently perform to generate (hyper-parameter, local loss) pairs. The experimental settings for this local optimizer are configurable via its constructor parameters: `base_estimator` (default: 'GBRT', options: 'GP', 'RF', 'ET' for surrogate model), `acq_func` (default: 'EI', options: 'LCB', 'PI', 'gp_hedge', 'EIps', 'PIps' for acquisition function), and `n_initial_points` (default: 5, number of random samples before BO starts). It is important to note that the aggregation and meta-learning aspects of FLoRA (e.g., central aggregator, unified loss surface construction methods like SGM, SGM+U, MPLM, or APLM) are not present in this optimizer's code. This file exclusively represents a single, independent local optimization strategy, which is only one part of the overall FLoRA architecture."

    "Title": "Single-shot General Hyper-parameter Optimization for Federated Learning",
    "Main Contributions": "The paper introduces Federated Loss SuRface Aggregation (FLoRA), a general single-shot hyper-parameter optimization (HPO) framework for federated learning (FL-HPO). It addresses the challenge of HPO in FL by enabling the identification of a single set of good hyper-parameters with minimal communication overhead. FLoRA is designed to be applicable to various Machine Learning (ML) models, including gradient boosting algorithms and tabular data, expanding the scope beyond neural networks. A key contribution is the theoretical characterization of FLoRA's optimality gap, explicitly accounting for the heterogeneous and non-IID nature of local data distributions. Empirical evaluation demonstrates significant model accuracy improvements over baselines and robustness to an increasing number of participating parties.",
    "Methodology": "FLoRA operates by leveraging meta-learning techniques and local, asynchronous HPO at each party. Each participating party independently runs an adaptive HPO scheme (e.g., Bayesian Optimization) to generate a set of (hyper-parameter, loss) pairs. These per-party sets are then collected by a central aggregator. The aggregator constructs a unified loss surface (a function mapping hyper-parameters to estimated loss) using one of four proposed aggregation methods: Single Global Model (SGM), SGM with Uncertainty (SGM+U), Maximum of Per-party Local Models (MPLM), or Average of Per-party Local Models (APLM). The optimal hyper-parameter configuration is then selected from this aggregated loss surface, and a single federated training round is performed using these recommended hyper-parameters to produce the final global model.",
    "Experimental Setup": "FLoRA was empirically evaluated using Histogram-based Gradient Boosted Decision Trees (HGB), Support Vector Machines (SVMs) with RBF kernel, and Multi-layered Perceptrons (MLPs) from scikit-learn. Experiments were conducted on seven binary classification datasets from OpenML, selected based on having at least 3% potential improvement in balanced accuracy for HGB. The number of parties was varied from 3 up to 100, with data randomly split across them. The baseline for comparison was the default hyper-parameter configuration of scikit-learn, which aligns with Auto-Sklearn defaults. Performance was measured using balanced accuracy, and relative regret was used to compare FLoRA against the baseline and a theoretical best centralized HPO. Some experiments emulated FL with centralized training for robust statistical evaluation, while others used the IBM FL testbed with a global hold-out validation set.",
    "Limitations": "The problem formulation in FLoRA simplifies certain aspects: it assumes no personalization (hyper-parameters are global across parties), defers HPO for aggregator-specific hyper-parameters for future work, and assumes a global holdout/validation set is available for final model evaluation but not for the HPO process itself. The theoretical analysis relies on Lipschitzness assumptions for loss functions and local loss surfaces, though it can be relaxed to modulus of continuity. The framework also assumes a fixed data distribution, not considering streaming data. While FLoRA shows robustness, its performance can degrade significantly when faced with extremely high heterogeneity across party data distributions (high 'gamma_p' ratio). Direct comparison with other existing FL-HPO schemes was not feasible due to their differing assumptions (e.g., focus on few hyper-parameters, server-side validation, personalization, neural networks, or weight-sharing capabilities not applicable to FLoRA's general scope).",
    "Future Research Directions": "Future research could extend FLoRA to include hyper-parameter optimization for aggregator-specific settings. Exploring strategies that involve coordination between parties during local HPO, beyond the current asynchronous approach, could also be a valuable direction. Additionally, investigating how to incorporate personalization by tuning per-party local hyper-parameters would enhance the framework's applicability. Adapting FLoRA for streaming data settings, where data distributions might change over time, is another potential area for expansion. Further work could also focus on developing methods to maintain performance gracefully in scenarios with extremely high data heterogeneity across federated parties.",
    "Experiment Code": null,
    "Experiment Result": null

{'novelty_reason': 'Most prior ‘curve-aware’ methods (BOIL, DPL, DyHPO, etc.) build a global surrogate over the joint space (h-params × budget) and therefore need Gaussian-process training, neural nets or ensembles plus acquisition-function optimization.  CPSH departs from this pattern: it keeps the original ASHA scheduler unchanged and substitutes a one-line local projection per worker (OLS fit of log-linear curve) to obtain a lower-confidence estimate.  No global model, no cross-configuration coupling, and only two running sums are stored.  None of the listed works combine (i) pure local curve extrapolation, (ii) confidence-adjusted pruning, and (iii) zero extra wall-time overhead, so the approach is genuinely new in its lightweight yet curve-aware design.', 'novelty_score': 7, 'significance_reason': 'Hyper-parameter search dominates GPU cost in practice; any drop-in change that reduces resources without hurting final accuracy is highly valuable.
